SUMMARY:
This report contains a description of farm operations on a farm in Guaa-
in 1943 and early 1944. New information includes the statement that farm peoplo,
except children under 7 and persons 60 years of age or older, were considered as
laborers when celculating the rice rations to be retained on the farm, above that
sold to the Government. In addition to the market-quality riee which the farmors
were allowed to keep, they also kept the cull rice for their own use. Whem the
1943 crop was harvested, the family hed left only a small amount of rice and
wheat in their private storage, but the Government storehouses located on their
farm were still about one-third full of rice. Rice was kept for a period of
months in the better storehouses such as theirs, and drawn out of the poorer
storehouses for distribution after a comparatively short time. Fertilizer

INTRODUCTION
The following report is based on interviews of M-92l, of Japanese national-
ity. He is the son of a Landlord-Farmer vho lived in O_NO-OAZA, HOSEN-MURA,
NITTA-GUN, GULDA-KEN. This is west of the town of OTA. M-921 was first indueted
July 26, 1943. After spend ing about 3 months in regular military training at
UTSUNOnIYA, he wes discharged October 23, 1943. He was recalled to military
service June 5, 1944. The family to which M-921 belonged apparently oceupied a
leading position in the community, and he wambetter informed than most farmers
would be concerning the cooperative and governmental organizations to which
Japanese fermers. belong. However, since he was not the head of the family, there
were some matters, such as taxes, on which he was not in possession of full
details. He talked freely and appeared to be cooperative. He was not in a
position to have knowledge concerning industrial plants in OTA except as to their
location, but there is a possibility that he did not tell all he knew on this
particular subject. The report is rated F-3.

The Government buys only good rice. The crop, in this case 128 hye, 1s

and broken grains are removed. These constitute something less than lo% of the

of rice to the family supply, making the total available about 34 hyo. (Interro-

other sources. The cull grain probably has approximately the same food value as
the good grain.)

Rice Quotas for 1944. M-92l remembered thet a national rice quota for the

was. He believed that the Government begen by raising the quota for the entire
nation and parceling out the increase first to prefectures, then to smaller
units, down to the individual farm. He believed that the quota for his father'
farm would be about the seme as it was in 1943. He based this belief, however,
mostly on the fact that in his opinion it would not be possible for his
family to grow any more rice; nor for them to consume any less.
In 1943, the farmers had delivered to the Government warehouse the amount .
of rice which they had in excess of that which was required for their own needs
at the retes previously noted. M-92l did not know of any plan to chenge this
procedure in 1944. The Government warehouse for the village was on his father?s
farm, and he was therefore in a good position to know about these matters.
JAPANESE AGRICULTURAL ORGANIZATIONS - RECENT CHANGES
M-92l said that in April 1944 the two principal agricultural organisations
in Japan, the NO KAI and the SANGYO KUi IAI, had been combined into one organ-
ization knovn as the NOGYO KAI. He described in considerable detail the
organization and functions of these two institutions. It is through them that
the Japanese Government menages the agricultural wer effort.
NO KAI. Before the amelgametion in April 1944, the NO KAI was organized
from the top down, as follows:
TEIGOKU NO KAI (This is headed by a peer.)
KEN NO KAI
GUN NO KAI
CHOSON NO KAI

ANGYO KUHIAI. M-921 said that the SANGYO KUMIAI, before the
versa.
The smallest division was the CHOSON SANGYO KUMIAI, which was divided into
the following four sections:
I. RYo (usage or rental)
II. KOBAI (purchasing)
III. HANBAI (selling)
IV. SHINYO (credit)
On the prefecturel level there were the following three organizations,
centralizing the activities of the corresponding tovmship organizations:
KEN KOBAI RENGOKAI
KEN HANBAI RENGOKAI
KEN SHINYO RENGOKAI
On the national level there were the folloving three organizations:
ZENKOKU KOBAI RENGOKAI (abbreviated as ZENKOBAI)
ZENKOKU HANBAI RENGOKAI (abbreviated as ZENKOHAI)
CHUO KINKO
The last organization, loceted in TOkYo, is essentially a central, bank.
At the township level the functions of the RYo were to rent out toole and
equipment to fermers. The function of the KOBAI was to purchase equipment,
fertiliser, seeds, and other f rm supplies. The HAnBAI formerly sold agricul-
tural commodities and acted to control agricultural prices, but M-92l belioved
that with national price control in effect they no longer performed any prico-
control function.
The function of SHInYo was to lend money to farmers and also to receive
savings deposits. Loans to farmers were limited to about ¥5,0oo per farmer.
These were not mortgege loans, but vere secured by the guarantor system; that
is, the borrower persuaded two or three guarantors to sign the note with him
(the note vas called a "SHAKUYO SHOSHO"). There were two forms of guarantee.
In one case each guerantor was responsible for the entire amount of the loan
if the others failed to pay. This system may be celled "Unlimited liability
guarantee". In the other case, each guarantor wes responsible for only 
certain proportion of the debt and could not be called upon to pay any more.
This may be called "Limited liability guarantee.
These loans were made for a period of three or four years. Interest was
calculated on a per diem basis. (M-92l remarked that in Japan interest paid

KANGYo GINko (Hypothec Bank of Jepan). This is a farm-mortgage bank, not
connected with the foregoing orgenizations, which lends money to farmers on real
estate security. One of its principel interests in assisting tenants to
purchase the land which they farm. -921 said that he believed this bank m
very active at the present time.
NOGYO KAI. The effect of combining the NO KAI and the SANGYO KUwTAI will
be merely to abolish the cooperative festures of these two agricultural organ-
izations and make them into stronger instruments for carrying out the war-time
policy of the Japanese Government. Under the present arrangement the line of
authority in agricultural affairs is as follows:
(A) Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry.
(B) Prefectural NOGYO KAI. This organization is headed by a member of
the Prefectural Government, who has to be a "powerful man".
(c) District Headquarters of the Prefectural Government, the head of
which is appointed by the Prefectural Government.
(D)
GUN NOGYO KAI.
(E)
CHOSON NOGYO KAI. At this level the elements of the NO KAI and of tho
SAIGYO KU IAI are both present. The head of the organization is
appointed by the Prefectural Government. The village couneil
nominete one man for the approval of the Prefectural Govornment,
but he will not be appointed unless he meets with their approval.
If a nominee is disapproved, another has to be offered.
(F) NOJI KUMIAI. There is one of these organizations in each BURAKU BLOK
which is a very small village sub-division usually containing 15 to
30 families. The leader of the NOJI KUaIAI is chosen from among
the farmers and is usually an educated person, a member of the
gentry. The father of M-92l was leader of the local NOJI KUTAI
up until 1943. The NOJI KUMIAI passes on to the individual farmer
the crop-acreage quotas, distributes fertilizer rations, and performs
similar duties related to agricultural control. It is the liaison
agent between the farmer end the CHOSON NOGYO KAI.
In the administration of agricultural affairs, orders usually come from tho
Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry to the Prefectural NOGYO KAI, past the
district headquarters of the Prefectural Government, past the GUN NoGYo KAI,
to the CHOSON NOGYO KAI and to the NOJI KUMIAI. The district headquarter of
the Prefectural Government and the GUN NOGYo KAI act as cooperating organizations
but not as directing organizations.
From JICA/CIB. New Delhi
Report No. R-125-D-45

-ACREAGE CONTROL
Only the staple crops are subject to acreage control. Rice,
barley, in that order, are considered the principal crops under 
followed by Irish potatoes, sweet potatoes, maise, soybeans, taro, md
(millet). The acreages of vegetables are not under control, exeept that
is provided that only a restricted acreage of melons can be grown.
The quotas are handed on down to the CHOSON NOGYO KAI from tho higher
authorities, and it determines the quotas for each staple crop which shall be
furnished by each NOJI KUMIAI. The NOJI KUMIAI in turn notifies each farmer
what percentage of his acreage he shall plant to each different crop.
RATIONING OF FERTILIZER
M-921 had charge of fertilizer distribution in his NOJI KUMIAI, tho BURAKU
farm organization, in 1942. He had to give up these duties in 1943 becauso of
his army-training period. The following informaticn on fertilizer rations, he
stated, applied to the wheat and barley crops harvested in the spring of 1943
and to the rice crop harvested in the autumn of 1943.• He could not speak for
the fertilizer rations allotted for the wheat crop harvested in the spring of
1944 or to be available for the rice crop harvested in the autumn of 1944.
Fertilizer purchase periods. The fertilizer year is divided into the first
period, April to August, and the second period, September to March. Fortiliser
purchased during the first period is to be applied on rice, and fertiliser
purchased during the second period, on wheat and barley.
No fertilizer for minor staple crops. Only the three principal staple crops,
rice, wheat, and barley, were provided any commercial fertilizer ration. The
other staple crops in the list beginning with Irish potatoes and ending with
millet, were not supposed to receive commercial fertilizer, but were to be for-
tilized with whatever home-produced material was available. M-92l said that
farmer could actually use alloted fertilizers for other than the intended
purposes if he wished, but if he did so he would have insufficient for his
staple crops.
Fertilizer is distributed a little at a time, and not all at the boginning
of the season.
Ammonium sulphate. Fertilizer rations were determined on the basis of the
number of tan of rice in the first period, or of wheet and barley in the second
period.
Ammonium sulphate retions per tan per year were as follows:
Rice - 15 kan (1 kan equals 3.75 kilograms.)
Wheat - 12 kan
This means thet on 1 tan of lend which grew rice in the summer season and
wheat in the winter season, a farmer could apply 27 tan.

Mixed fertilizer. This fertilizer, manufactured by SumItouo, was rationed
as follows, per ten per year:
Rice - 2 kan

It contained nitrogen, phosphoric acid, and potash in proportions not

Other fertilizers. Soybean cake and fish meal were available in all
amounts, but there wes not enough to provide a regular ration. Another ferti-
lizer was called "Thomas Rimpi". by M-92l. "Rimpi" is an abbreviation for
Rinsan Hiryo, that is, phosphate fertilizer. (Interrogator's comment: This
material is undoubtedly whet is knovn as "Thomas Slag", a by-product of the
basic Bessemer process of steel manufacture.) M-92l said thet the material oon-
tained 16% P,05 and was issued as a substitute for calcium superphosphate.
Very little of it was available, but if a farmer purchased any Thomas Rimpe
his ration of calcium superphosphate was reduced correspondingly. He aid that
the Thomas Rimpe could not be mixed with other fertilizer components, such as
ammonium sulphate. He did not know why, because he had followed diroctions and
never mixed them.
Distribution of fertilizer. When purchasing fertilizer, the farmer,
through the NOJI KUMIAI, gets a certificate from the CHOSON NOGYO KAI. He then
takes the certificate to the fertilizer dealer end obtains the material. The
fertilizer dealer obtains his supply from the ken NOGYO KAI, which, in turn,
obtains it from fertilizer manufacturers through the national NOGYO KAI.
The only fertilizer material which can be bought and sold commercially with-
out restriction is human waste from the towms.

-10-
ffect of fertilizer ration on crop vields. It was not possible to obtaim
from M-921 a olear cowparison between the amounts of fertilizer used by his
father after rationing went into effect and the amounts used previously. Ro
stated that before rationing his father used mainly mixed chemioal fertiliser,
while under rationing the unmixed fertilizer materials, previously listed, were
purchased and mixed. He believed that the quality of fertiliser used by
farmers had improved considerably since rationing went into effect, because the
mixed fertilizers sold before rationing were often low in quality. (Interroga-
tor's comment: There may be considerable truth in this. Mixed fertilisers
which are sold to fermers ordinarily contain considerable inert material, called
"filler", and the farmer must pay for the purchase, mixing, storage, and trans-
portation of this material. Pre-mixed fertilizers also often contain materials
which are neerly insoluble even though they are included as fertiliser elemonts
in chemical analysis. When farmers purchase the unmixed ingredients and mix
them on th ferm, considerable economy usually results and the fertilising power
per pound of fertilizer as applied is likely to be higher.)
M-92l made the interesting statement that because of the extreme shortage of
potash fertilizer, a limit hed had to be placed on the application of nitrogen
fertilizer. If as much nitrogen as formerly had been applied, without the
usual quantity of potash, the gre in stocks would have been weak and the grain
would have lodged before harvest. Limiting the nitrogen fertilizer naturally
limited the crop. He believed thet if sufficient potash fertilizer had been
available, so that the usual amount of nitrogen could have been applied, crops
would have been as large as pre-wsr.
He stated that if fertilizer had not been limited in any way the yield of
rice would have been 9 to l0 hyo per tan, instead of the 8 hyo which was
actually harvested. A yield of 9 or 10 hyo per tan was usual on this farm before
the wer.
(Interrogetor's co ment: If 9.5 hyo per tan is taken as the pre-ration
yield, it appears that yields have been reduced about 10% due to the shortage of
fertilizer.
The fertilizer rations given by -92l are considerably larger in most
respects than the fert ilizer r tions given by M-927, E.I.S. Office Memo No. 216,
JICA No. R-121-D-45, vhose ferm vaε in IBARAKE-KEN. It is quite possible that
both men told the truth, and that fert ilizer rftions do differ in different-type-
of-farming areas. M-927 lived in a section which produced comparatively littlo
rice and where crop yields were lover than on the ferm of M-92l. The difference
in yield is undoubtedly due to a difference in soil. M-927 lived in a sandy-
soil erea.)

a limit to the percentege of income which can be taken for a towm and ville
tax, but M-921 could not remember it.
So far as he could remember, these taxes were collected in April and im
August.
He believed it was true that the land was taxed twice,, once by the prefee-
ture and once by the National Government.
The forests are taxed by the prefecture. The rate is one-tenth of the
rate for agricultural land.
COLLECTION AND STORAGE OF RICE BY THEGOVERNMENT
As previcusly stated, grain verehouses of the NOJI KUMIAI were situated on
the farm of M-921. There were two of these buildings, 6 ken long, 5 ken deop.
11.5 shaku tall. (1 ken equals 1.99 yards. 6 shaku equals 1 ken.) Each of
these werehouses holds 500 hyo of rice, and M-921 said that the rice was piled
7 bags deep. (1 bag equals 1 hyo.)
The new crop vas put in the warehouse in the form of hulled (brown) rice.
Just after the rice was p cked in, the varehouses were fumigated with one litre
of chloropicrin. This was sufficient for both warehouses. About June, the
warehouzes were again opened and fumigated with the same amount of chloropiorin.
The walls of these verehouses are dirt sheethed with wood, and the ware-
houses were considered very good. For this reason the Government allowed the
rice to be stored in these warehouses for a comparatively long time, using the
rice from other warehouses first. In the autumn of 1943, before the new rieo
crop was put in, these two warehouses were still about one-third full of ice.
The rice had begun to be shipped out of these warehouses in September. After
the 1943 crop had been put in, the warehouses were sealed, and they had not yet
been re-opened to take cut any rice by the time he was recalled to the Army on
June 5, 1944. The warehouse of the CHOSON NOGYO KAI was built in 1941. Thi
was 15 to 16 ken long, about 8 ken wide, and about 15 shaku tall, a one-storey
building enclosing one large room. M-92l could not report on the amount of
rice left in this storehouse et the time of the 1943 harvest nor later. He
said he had not been in it.
M-92l did not know who manufactured the chloropicrin used in fumigation.
(Interrogator's note: On one occasion during 1944, the Japanese sent a ship-
ment of chloropicrin to SAIGON to fumigate 5,Oo0 tons of rice held there in
the MITSUBISHI warehouses.)
From JICA/CIB, New Delhi
Report R-125-D-45
26 January 194

AIRCRAFT FACTORIES
M-921 could give no informetion of consequence on aircraft plant in ora
and vicinity. The only aircraft plant that he knew of in OTA was the makAJm
plant, located just east of OTA town, as indicated on Map M-4 of Air Objoctive
Folder No. 90.12-13, July 24, 1944, and on the Emergency Edition Map of the

which he knew is the one listed as 1544 on Map M-4. The last time he passed
the plant was in May 1943. He could see very little, because the compound was
surrounded by a concrete wall 10 shaku high. Nobody in his family worked in
the NAkAJIMA factories. Those who lived in the village and worked thore
left for the factory on bicycles et 6 a.m. and came back at 5 or 6 p.m.,

His village wes ebout 5 kilometers from the plant.

paddy fields.
He had never been to KOIZUI.

no lerge factories there, only small textile fectories.
He believed thet the airport was south of OTA, but had never seen it.
Many planes were seen flying over his neighborhood. There were several types,
including planes of 1, 2, and 4 motors. All were monoplanes.
UTSUNOMIYA
M-92l had never heard of any new Army Air Forces arsenal being built near
UTSUNOuIYA. He believed there was an air training school somewhere north of
UTSUNOMIYA, but had never seen it.

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
NATURAL RESOUROES SBCTION
REPORT MUMBER 59
15 October 1946
THE AGRICULTURAL EXPERIMENT STATIONS OF JAPAN
(A Preliminary Report)
SUMMARY
1. In relation to area of cultivated land and agricultural income,
Japan has one of the most extensive systems of agricultural research in
the world. This research is conducted principally by the Imperial and
prefectural agricultural experiment stations and also by the Imperial
universities, a number of colleges, private institutions, and corporations.
They have played an important role in making it possible for Japan to
increase agricultural production greatly in recent years.
2. The egricultural experiment stations of Japan are relatively mall
and numerous. This appears to be due to a tendency to establish mall
independent stations with limited responsibilities instead of large
stations with multiple duties as in the United States. Also a wide
diversity of climate makes it desirable to have many stations to serve
relatively small areas..
3. The over-all objectives of Japanese agricultural research have
been to increase production, improve the quality of agricultural products,
and decrease the costs of production. Because of a large population and
scarcity of arable land, increasing the yields per unit area of land has
been regarded as of paramount importance.
4. In order to achieve these objectives, specific researches and
experiments have been conducted with practically every science relating to
agriculture.
5. In general, the Imperial experiment stations devote their main
effort to the discovery of basic principles and the solution of problems of
national importance. The prefectural stations generally center their
attention on local problems with emphasis on results of immediate practical
value. However, considerable basic research is conducted at these
stations.
6. Co-ordination of effort appears to be good, and little unnecessary
or undesireble duplication of work occurs between the Imperial and the
prefectural stations. Co-ordination of the work of the stations of the
different prefectures is not so evident except as it has been affected
through co-operation with the Imperial stations. The number of livestock
stations seems to be in excess of actual neede.
7

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.98)
7. A few of the more important achievements of prectical value of
the agricultural experiment stations include the creation of new and
superior races or varieties of silkworms and rice, new methods of caring
for end rearing silkworms, marked decreases in costs of harvesting tea
leaves and in curing them, control of diseases and insect pests. and
studies of the relative yields of varieties of rice and wheat with and
without fertilisers.
8. An outstanding contribution is the use of B1 hybrid silkworms
for the production of silk. Specific methods for using them developed
as a result of experiments conducted by the Imperial Sericultural
Experiment Station. Other notable achievements of this station are the
treating of silkworm eggs, the development of methods of storage, and
artificial hatching so that monovoltine races may be hatched any time of
the year. As a result of these and other improvements due in part to
research, the production of cocoons per unit area of mulberries increased
about 150 percent and of silk more than 400 percent between 1890 and
1940. Production of silk per metric ton of cocoons increased more than
100 percent during the same period.
9. Rice, the most important crop in Japen, has been the subject of
extensive investigation. A large number of new varieties adapted to
particular regions or for special conditions have been produced. It has
been extimated that about 69 percent of the rice area grows varieties
produced and distributed by the agricultural experiment stations. A
special feature of these are early maturing varieties that make it
possible to grow rice on paddy land in practically all parts of Hokkaido.
10. Wheat occupied a relatively minor position in Japan until the
early 1930s. A five-year plan inaugurated in 1932 by the Imperial
Government resulted in an increase in acreage and marked increases in
yield per unit area. Chief among the reasons for the latter are early
maturing disease resistant varieties produced and distributed by the
Imperial and prefectural agriculturel experiment stetions. These
varieties now total 19, each of which is grown on one percent or more of
the wheat area. Together they are rown on about 66 percent of the wheat
erea.
11. Development of methods of controlling plant diseases has been
an important function of the egricultural experiment stations. Breeding
resistant varieties has been a favorite method but is not applicable or
feasible with respect to all diseases. Soil sanitation, seed treatment,
spreying plants with fungicides, end roguing ere other generel methods
for which specific procedures have been developed. Few importsnt
diseases exist in Japan for which effective methods have not been devised
larcely as a result of research.
12. The yields per unit area of rice, wheat, common barley, and
neked barley have increased about 70, 140, 119, and 62 percent,
8

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.99)
respectively, in the past 60 years, using as bases for comperison the
average yields for the five-year per1ods of 1878-82 and 1938-42. The
increases for rice and wheat are all the more remarkable because they
have been accompanied by an increase in the acreage of rice of 25 percent
and of wheat of ll9 percent. They are believed to be due to the more
extensive use of commercial fertilisers and of better varieties singly
and in combination.
13. A significant discovery in this connection is that improved
varieties often are no better than others with inadequate quantities of
fertilizer, and alsc that the best results with fertilisers will not be
obtained if improved varieties are not grown. Both are necessary for
maximum yields.

WATURAL RESOURCES SECTION
REPORT NUMBER 59
15 October 1946
THE AGRICULTURAL EXPERIMENT STATIONS OF JAPAN
INTRODUCTION
A. General
1. Few countries surpass Japan in the efficient use arable
land 1/, or in the increased productior per unit area that has been
accomplished in recent years. Although Japan has more than one-half the
population of the United States and less than one-twentieth es much
cultivated land, she has been alle to produce from 80 to 85 percent of
ner domestic food requirements as well as considerable quentities of non-
food crops for export either as raw materials or as menufectured goods.
Yields per unit area of the important food crops (rice, wheat, and barley),
have been almost doubled since about 1880. Production of silk per hectare
of mulberry trees has increased more than 500 percent during the same
period. The agriculturel experiment stations heve played en important
role in making these improvements possible.
2. A preliminary eppraisal of the agricultural experiment
stations of Japan has been made to ascertain whether the number, location,
organization, physicel facilities, personnel, and budgets are such as to
assure the greatest possible essistance in maximazing the production of
food and critical raw materials in accordance with the objectives of the
Occupation. Since this study cannot be regarded as complete, any con-
clusions or opinions should be considered tentative.
B. Agricultural Reseerch Institutions of Jaran
1. Agricultural research in Japan is conducted by many public
and private institutions or agencies. The most importent research insti-

3. Japan does not have any formally organised institutions
whose primary function is adult education of farmers or vocational

the responsibility for whatever is done in Japan along these lines fells
upon the experiment station systen.
THE JAPANESE AGRICULTURAL EXPERIMNT STATIONS
A. History
1. The present system of agricultural experiment stations in
Jepan dates from 1890 when the Imperial agricultural experiment station
was established at Nishigahare in Tokyo to conduct research with field.
fruit, and vegetable crops (Figure 1). .Six branch stations were added
in 1893 and three more in 1896. Six of these were discontinued in 1903
coincident vith the esteblishment of the prefectural stations which were
expected to assume their functions. Two more were discontinued in 1924.
Later additions were made end Japan now has nine brench stations. These
include the Konosu Experimental Farm which functions as a field station
for the main station at Nishigehera, which has no land for field experi-
ments (Table 1)
2. Facilities for resesrch in animal husbandry were provided
by the estaulishment of the Imperiel Zootechnical Experiment Station at
Chiba, Chiba Prefecture, in 1916 end four branch stations in other

term in a restricted sense in accorasnce with Japanese usage to

3. The agricultural experiment stations of Japan may be classi-
fied according to whether they are (1) administered by the Imperial or
prefectural government; (2) owmed by private individuals or corporations;
(3) devoted to general type of agricultural research; and (4) classified
as main stations, branch or substations, experimental farms, service
establishments, or research laboratories (Table 7).
4. The classification is necessarily somewhat arbitrary because
of duplication of functions in many cases, and because the distinctions
are not clear in others. So far as possible the classification is in
accord with the official designation of each station. A prefectural
"agricultural experiment stetion", for example, may be included under
"crops" even though it may conduct some research with livestock. Many
subsidiary stations are aesignated as "branch stations" and "experimental
or substations" by the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, and they
are so classified here. On the other hand, the Ministry does not desig-
nate "service" stations as such. The term is used here to include
stations whose primary function appears to be to educate or inform
farmers or to perform direct services for them.
5. In eenerel, main stations are edministrative centers of the
lmperial or prefectural governments for research dealing with each agri-
cultural industry. Usually each prefecture has an Agricultural Experi-
ment Station, & Sericultural Experiment Station. and & Livestock Ferm.
There may also be a Horticultur l Experiment Station or more Eenerally en
Apple Experiment Station or a Citrus Experiment Station Such stations
have been included with the main stations vhen administratively inde-
pendent of others in the prefecture. The sane is true of tea stations
and tobacco stations, except that they are cla sified as industrial
rather than es horticulturel or generel stations.
6. The research conducted at the brench stations and substetions
is elong the same lines as thet of the main stations but on a emeller
scale. The experimental farms ere not only saaller then branch stations
but are charged with fewer responsibilities. Often they are limited to
e single crop or to a special problem of limited scope.

branch, and substations; experimental farms, service stations, and
research laboratories serve agriculture in Japan (Teble 7). Excluding

C. Land Area of Agricultural Experiment Stations
1. The area of land available for research for all main,
branch, service stations and experimental farms, for which data are
available is shown in Table 8.
2. Stations with 10 hectares or less of land greatly pre-
dominate. Five hectares or less is the model size for other than the
crops main stations. Many of the livestock stations are limited to
poultry or small animals which require only mall areas of land. The
predominance of small stations emphasizes the tendency of the Japanese
Government to set up special stations for particular purposes rather
than single lerge stations with multiple responsibilities as in the
United States.
3. Even the main stations are much smaller than corresponding
establishments in the United States. Nowhere in Japan does one find
10-, 20-, or 40-acre fields devoted to crop rotation, fertiliser, and
crop sequence experiments; or from 5,000 to 10,000 varieties and strains
of a single crop being tested for yield and resistance to diseases; or
extensive feeding tests of cattle or swine involving more than a few
heads. Land for such purposes in Japan is limited and for various
reasons is not needed to the same extent as in the United States. Nost
of the field work is done by hand which means that plots and experi-
mental fields are amall. In comparison with Japanese farms (average
size 1.0 hectare or about 2.5 acres) the stations ere larger, that is,
thoy are equivalent to a larger number of farms than are those of the
United States.

H. Budgets
1. The average annual budget of the Imperial and prefectarel
agricultural experiment stations for the 1936-40 and 194l-45 periods are
shown in Table 13. Increases in wages, salaries and other expenses
probably absorbed much of the increases in budgets for the latter peried
although no data bearing directly on this point have been collected.
2. Direct comparison with expenditures for agrioulture research
in the United States is difficult because of differences in responsi-
bilities as well as in wages, salaries, and other expenses. In general,
wages and salaries in yen in Japan were less than comparable items in
dollers in the United States. It appears that in relation to areas of
cultivated land and probably also in relation to agrioultural income;
Japan spent considerably more for agricultural research than did the
United States during these periods.
I. Freedom of Research
1. Interviews and casual examinations of Japanese sciontific
literature indicate that employees of the Imperial universities have a
surprising degree of freedom in choosing subjects for research as well
as in methods of approach. This appears to be true in many cases even
to the extent that colleagues in the same laboratory and the adminis-
trative officer in charge may not be informed as to what is being done.
2. Freedor of choice is not so evident in the agricultural
experiment stations. Most projects appear to have been planned in
relation to others in the same institution and to other institutions
belonging to the same subject matter classification. Where such projects
are of nation-wide significance and importance, conformity with the over-
all plan seems to be expected. Aside from this the leaders apparently
have as much choice with respect to individual research projects as would
be expected in the United States.

1. Although theoretically independent of each other, consider-

prefectural experiment stations, expecially in problems of national
importance or of particular interest to two or more prefectures. For
example, Tables 14 and 15 indicate respectively the prefectural stations

to considerable unnecessery duplication of physical fecilities and of

prefecture would effect considerable reduction in expenses for upkeep.
make it possible to have better facilities and better trained personnel,

D. Methods of Research
1. The over-all methods and philosophy of research appear to
be substantially the same as in the United States. In research with.
field crops, relatively more attention is given to laboratory research
and less to field experiments, possibly because land for the latter is
limited. This means relatively more dependence is placed on deductions
from general principles believed to have been established, and less on
empirical trials; also that much attention is given to establishing
principles. Nuch work has been done on the development of special
methods that will provide information on the adaptation of different
varieties and cultural practices to specific localities. Some of these
are reminiscent of similar developments in the United States; others are
new or quite different. Some of the modifications in breeding technics
are of general interest.
2. A few of the developments in methods, facilities, and
technics designed to furnish information to serve as a basis for better
practices or for breeding new varieties are (l) special experiment
stations end facilities for determining the response of rice varieties
to unseasonably low temperature; (2) the response of varieties of rice
to cold water; (3) a series of stations with environments and facilities
suitable for studying the effect of deficient sunshine on yields of rice
and of different varieties of rice; (4) special environments favorable
to the pericularia disease of rice in order to facilitate the selection
of resistant varieties; (5) provisions for raising and lowering the
water table in order to determine the effect of deficient water supplies
on the yield of rice and the response of different varieties thereto;
(6) means for determining the response of varieties of wheat and of rice
to length of day; (7) a potassium chlorate injury test for preliminary
evaluation of the resistarce of varieties and selections of wheat to
freezing temperatures; (8) special stations for determining the
resistance of varieties and selections of barley and wheat to snow
24

=== Page 53 === (confidence 0.99)
blight and the relation of damage to depth of snow, period snow lies on
the ground. carbohydrate exhaustion of the plants, and eusceptibility to
specific disease organisms; (9) methods for determining seed dormancy or
post harvest sprouting of varieties and selections of wheat, and (10)
selected stations and special methods for determing the resistance of rice
varieties to the rice borer.
3. Study of the principles that govern the adaptation of varie-
ties of wheat and rice to particular localities has been emphasised. For
this purpose the distribution of native varieties and of new varieties
adapted to restricted areas only, have been studied with respect to vari-
ous plant characteristics and the relation of these to specific climatic
and soil conditions and to disease and insect pests. Of special interest
are current studies on the response of wheat and barley varieties to soil
acidity and to deficiencies of specific mineral elements, especially
phosphorous and potassium.
4. The extensive practice of distributing F3 and F4 generations
of wheat and rice to subsidiary or co-operating stations appears to be
unique. In the United States hybrid populations ordinarily are not re-
leased to other stations until selections have been made, purified, and
subjected to preliminary tests. In Japan early distribution of new
selections is regarded as an important and integrel part of the breeding
program. Since the procedure is substantially the same for both wheat
and rice, it will be described only for wheat.

E. Field Plot Techniques
1. Few date have been collected regaraing rield plot techniques.
Such observations as it hes been possible to make, interviews, rd exami-

received little attention. Experiments are onerally conducted in single,
duplicate, or occasionally in triplicate plot tests. In meny cases.
however, variety tests and perheps others are repcated on different fields
with different fertilizers, dates, or ratcs of secding. Also the same
experiment may be repeated in substantially the seme way at several
stations.
2. Plots are generally smell. The usual sizes for small grain
plots appear to be 5 to 10 tsubos (approximately 20 to 40 square yards).
So-called field experiments such as used in the United States with plots
large enou h to be seeded, and harvested with horse or power-drewn
equipment are prectically unknown in Japen. The grein is seeded by hand,
harvested with a sickle, end threshed with a flail or a simple threshing
machine consisting of a wooden cylinder with looped-wire teeth end
opereted by a motor.

A. General
1. Because several years would be required to assemble the
necessary information and interpret the achievements of the various
experiment stations, the discussion will be confined to only a few con-
tributions which appear to have resulted in outstanding practical beme
fits.
B. Improvements in Silk Production
1. Improvements in silk production in Japan provide en exemple
of an outstanding contribution of science to an agricultural industry as
well as useful avenues of approach. Probably no Japanese industry is so
dependent on research as is the silk industry, and certainly none has
profited more from research.
2. The sericultural experiment stations, which have the major
responsibility of research in this field, have investigated practically
all problems related to the silk industry. The studies that have con-
tributed to the most importent practical results include: (1) selection
and breeding of superior races or varieties of silkworms and of
mulberries; (2) relation of environmentel conditions to voltiam; (3)
nutrition and rearing methods for silkworms: (4) control of disease and
insect pest of mulberries and silkworms; (5) improvement in the methods
of testing silkworm moths for the presence of the pebrine disease; (6)
economical methods of. producing silkworm eggs and of increasing the
supply for distribution to farmers; (7) improvements in filature
practices; (8) methods of gathering and feeding leaves to reduce losses
from rapid drying while simultaneously avoiding losses of silkworms
from diseases favored by high humidity; and (9) culture of mulberries
including varieties, methods, and rates of fertilisation.
3. A notable achievement is the use of I1 hybrids between
certain Chinese and Japanese races of sill:orms now used almost ex-
clusively for the commercial production of silk in Japan (Figure 9)
These Fi hybrids are more vigorous, less susceptible to disease, and
spin larger cocoons than any pure race of silkworms. The same basic
genetic principle (heterosis) is involved in silkworm hybrids as in the
prbduction of hybrid corn in the United States. The basic discoveries
leading to the use of F1 hybrids in silk production were made by K.
Toyama of Tokyo Imperial University in 1909 8/and put to use about five
years later. The sharp upward trend in silk production per metric ton
reported elsewhere.

7. Simple but effective improvements in feeding techniques
prevent excessive drying of the young mulberry leaves has resulted im
saving of about 20 percent. The improved method consits of feedingt
young worms in boxes covered with paraffin-impregnated paper to maintain
the humidity at mar the saturation point. At the same time, mildew and
other diseases are prevented by spraying with formalin. After the secemd
instar, the leaves are fed attached to the branches which prevents eze
cessively rapid drying and saves much labor otherwise expended in picking
the leaves. Because of these improvements, it has been estimated that the
over-all saving in labor will be not less than 40 percent.
8. A recent improvement in methods is the development of co-
operative rearing of the worms for the first 10 days after hatching.
During this period they require special care to prevent injury by un-
favorable weather. The co-operative service provide? controlled temper-
ature and moisture conditions, specially trained help and technical
advice. The method is comparable to the mass hatching of baby chicks by
commercial enterprises in the United States, except that it has been
developed along co-operative lines in Japan.

C. Improvement of Rice Varieties
1. Because rice is the most important crop in Japan, it has
been the subject of more intensive and extensive investi ation than any
other crop. The experiments thet appear to heve been particularly
productive are those on the efficient use of fertilizers, especially the
interrelations of fertilizers and varieties; and those on the impro vement
of varieties by breeding including those concerned with basic principles.
While this discussion will be limited to varicty improvement, it should
be noted that best results are obteined with superior varieties only on
properly fertilized soils and, conversely, the best rcsults with ferti-
lizers cannot be expected unless suitable varieties are rown.
2. Since rice has been grown in Japen for meny hundreds of
years, considerable inprovement in verieties would le expected as a
result of natural selection as well as from conscious or unconscious
selection by farmers. vidence shows improvements have been effected by
all these methods. In a collection of more than 4,C00 local lots
assembled by the Imperial Agricultural xperiment Ststion some 45 yeers
ago, about 700 different types were found thet could be distin uished by
31

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.98)

3. That farmers have contributed to variety improvemems is
attested by the fact that 17 varieties produced by them were being srowm
as 1ate a 194510/.
4. The earliest improvemente for which definite records and
experimentel proof exist were made by selection from native varieties
both by farmers and by the agricultural experiment stations. One of the
best known of these varieties grown at the present time is Asahi selected
by a farmer in l903. It is now grown on about 11 percent of the rice.
area of Japan, principally in centrel Honshu. Other varieties produced
by fermers are Shinriki, selected in 1877; Aikoku, selected in 1889; and
Bozu, selected in 1895. Aikokn is grown on about four percent of the
rice acreage and the others to a lesser extent.
5. Riku-l32 was selected oy the Imperial Branch Tohoku station
because of its resistence to the pericularia disease. It was first dis-
tributcd in 1925 and is now one of the important varieties in northern
Honshu. It wes considered such a veluable variety by rice growers of
the area that a monument wes erected at Akita to comnemorate the avent.
6. An important forward step wes taken with the introduction
of foreign varieties, the discovery of resistance to pericularia in
certain ones, and the use of these verieties in crosses with native
varieties by the Imperial Agriculturel Experiment Station beginning about
1903. Since pericularie is the most important disease of rice in Japan,
the creation of varieties resistant to it has been an important objective
since that tine.
7. Serious deleys and difficulties were at once encountered
beceuse of (1) a hi h degree of sterility and (2) uncertainty in identi-
fying resistant se, recates because of sporadic occurrences of the
disease. The first difficulty hes been largely overcome by recrossing
and porsistent selection, end the second by rowing rice in special
environments favorable to the development of the diseese. As a result,
several new pericularia-resistant varieties have been produced in recent
years that are adepted for growin in various regions of Japan. The
first of th ese mede its appearance on Jepanese farms in 1930, and the
acrcn e devoted to thew has increased rapidly since that time. It has
been estimated that in 1946 about 46 percent of the total rice area of
Japen grew new hybrid verieties nd an additionel 23 percent grew

E. Plant Disease Control
1. In order to provide practical control measures, research with
plant diseases has been im important function of the agricultural experi-
ment stations for many years 12/.
2. A list of the principal diseases of field crops, together
with the names of the causal organiams generally accepted by Japanese
12 Prepared by Lt Cmdr E.L. Waldee, USNR, Agriculture Division
35

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.99)
authorities, is given in Table 23. Estimated losses owing to the more
important diseases for the three major crops for each year from 194l to
1945 inclusive are siven in Table 24.
3. Only two diseases will be discussed, i. e., wheat stripe,
and snow blight of wheat and barley. Both are of interest to the United
States. Wheat stripe has been found in the United States, but apparently
it is caused by a different organism. It is much less destructive than
the Japanese form. Snow blight damage is much more. general and hes been
studied more intensively in Japan than in the United States.
4. Wheat stripe first made its appearance in Okayama Prefecture
in 1931. Within three years it was found to be prevalent in 3,000
hectares and had spread to 5,000 hectares in this prefecture by 1941.
Control measures worked out by experiment stetion pathologists and put
into effect reduced the effected area to 2,000 hectares in 1944. In the
meantime the disease had spread to other areas of Japan as shown in
Figure 14.
5. The wheat stripe disease resembles barley stripe, being
caused by a similar organism (Cephalosporium gramineum Nish et Ikata).
It lives in the soil in plant debris and may be carried to new areas by
infected seed, straw, or animal droppings. Occurrence of the disease in
one prefecture in Japan is thought to have been the result of shipping
in infected shrunken grain for chicken feed. The disease occurs particu-
larly on upland fields. In paddy fields the disease organisms do not
ordinerily survive flooding. Infected fields produce either little or
no grain, or,badly shrunken grain only.
6. Control measures consist of (1) the use of disease-free
seed, or immersion of infected seed in a fungicide solution; (2) roguing
diseased plents; (3) avoiding the use of compost containing infected
straw; and (4) crop rotation. In Nagano Prefecture late seeding also is
recommended.
7. Snow blight (Figure l5) or snow damage occurs especially in
a long narrow belt of variable width in western Honshu extending almost
throughout the length of the island. Its occurrence is associated with
the presence of deep snow (50 cm or more) that lies on the ground for
80 or more days. Water from melting snow may be a contributing factor
where drainage is not adequate. The areas where damage is most severe
and frequent are shówn in Figure 16. In some areas damage may occur
nearly every year end in others only occasionelly. In certain areas
where damage is most severe little wheat or barley is grown.
8. Damage is due to fungus infection following carbohydrate
exhaustion of the plants. Fungi involved are Typhula itoana, Fusurium
spp, and Pythium spp. Two or more of the organisms mey be involved in
particular cases, but usually one will predominate. Typhula is

end prefectural governments have apparently been well aware of the besic

2. To an otserver from the United States, the most striking
feeture of the Jepanese systen is the lerge nuuber of experiment stations.
The question naturally arises as to whether so meny are neceseary. Among
the fects to be considered ars the wide renee of climatic conditions in
Jepan, the division of responsibilities of the experiment stations, the
tendency to establish several small stations rether than e single large
one, end the role of the prcfecturel stations in farmer educetion.
3. Fron the norticrn tip of Hok:aido to southern Kyushu is
a distence of more then 1, 100 miles and a range in latitude of more than
14°. Merked differences i altitude ere illustrated by Mount Fuji, which
rises to an elevation of about 12,400 feet. Northern Honshu and Hokkeido
are greatly effected in wintcr by cold winds fron Siberia which pick up
moisture frow the Japan Sea na deposit it as a heavy blsnket of snow
elong the coest and adjoinin hi hland. In contrest, southern Japan has
e subtropical climate similar in many respecte to that of the southeestern
United States. The average annual precipitation ran es fror fewer than
40 inches in easters Hokkaido to more than l20 inches in parts of Honshu
and Shikoku. Marked differences in tempersture and cloudiness are found
in different parts cf Japan in summer. Altogether, few countries have
such reat contrasts in climate es are found in such ehort diatances as
in Japen. This means that any experiment stations are necessary in
order to serve all agricultural artes properly.
4. Another reason for nuserous stetions is a tendency on the
part of the Japanese to set up slall stations for specirl protlens end
sin le crops rather than a sinic stations with ultiple responsibilities.
In sone cases this practice is clearly justified, as when a particular
locetion is chosen to study snow damege of wheet or barley in a place
where sno: damace occurs; or one to breed sweet potatoes because the
climate or soil of the main station is not suitcble for growin: this crop.
Whether equally co ent reasons epply in ell cr ses may well be doubted.
The advisability of setting up seperate and independent prefecturel
stations responsiule only to higher prefecturel officials for the study
of citrus fruits, horse-radish, or tobacco is also doubtful. A second
question, therefore, arises es to how much division of responsibility is
desirable.
5. Another inportant fect bearing on the nuwter of stations is
the resconsibility of the prefectural stetions for fermer education,
spocial services, and a certain amount of student training. These
functions are performed in the United States by separete institutions set
up for that purpose and by private individuals or corporations. Rural
travel in Japen is mostly by foot or by bicycle. Meny communities are
more or less isolated by mounteins and poor roads. In order to perform

these various services, a considerable number of prefectural stations is
necessary.
6. The existence of so many separate independent establishments
in each prefecture for agricultural research herdly seems justified.
Combining the crops, livestock, and sericulturel stations under the
general direction of a single technically trained administrator with
technically trained subordinates in charge of each importent subject
matter branch miht be expected to result in better administration, more
productive research, better trained personnel, and better equipped labo-
ratories and greenhouses. Congregation of students in one placewith
opportunities to become familiar with technical improvements in all
phsses of agriculture should result in a broader education and increased
opportunities for service. The number of livestock stations appears to
be excessive whether considered in reletion to the neads of the livestock
incustry or the opportunities for significant contributions to the
economy of J..pen (Fi ure 22).
7. Some reorganization or better means for liaison seems neces-

B. Relation of the Stations to Future Food Production
1. The question naturally arises es to whether the agriculturai
experiment stations can contribute to the current emergency; if so, how;
and whether they are doing all that cen reasonably be expected of them.
The first question can without doubt be answered in the affirmative. The
others require some explenation.
2. Because per wit area production is now hiher probably than
in any other country, the assumption seems prevalent that they can be no
higher. This appears to be an unwarranted assumption, provided adequate
quantities of fertilizers are made available. Fertilizers are so
importent that a discussion of the possibility of hi her yields is lergely
academic when based on eny other premise. However, the stetions can
provide valueble information as to the most effective use of inadequete
quantities of fertilizers.
3. The production per unit area curves (Tables 20, 21, 25, 26,
27, and Figures 10, 13, 18, end 21) were generally on the upgrade until
deficient supplies of fertilizers and possibly of labor owing to the war
began to have an effect. No good reason to assume they hed reached their
ultimate limits is ap arent. On the contrary it is well kmown thet with
favorable conditions, yields far in excess of the average yields for
Japan as a whole are possible. The problem, therefore, is one of avoidin
or removing the unfavorable factors or of reducine their effects.
4. Some of these unfavorable factors are related to weather or
climate which cannot be modifiei by man. It is sometimes possible, how-
ever, to grow crops or varieties that will avoid or escape their effects,
or be tolerant to them, or some of the effects mey be offset by special
management practices. For example, a deficient supply of weter at heading
time may seriously reduce the yield of rice. A relatively small quantity,
supplied perhaps by pumping underground water, would meterially increase
the yields under such conditions. In many small areas it appears
additionel water would be beneficial. Until the subject is investigated
more thoroughly, one can only speculte as to increase in production that
might reasonably be expected by this means.
45

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.98)
5. Other factors that reduce yielus ere insect and disease
pests. Lotable proress in controlling then has been made, but it is
not reasonchle to suppose that all possible or precticel measures have
been used.
6. Inadequate drainage in certei. freas reduces the yields or
oven prevents the seeding of a winter crop of wheat or berley efter rice.
Here also opportunities for improvement seem to exist.
7. Floods cause serious losses in some years 16/. It is not
clear that all practical measures have been taken to prevent such losses.
8. The Japenese people prefer rice to any other crop for food.
Hundreds of yecrs of experience in growing it and a certain degree of
conservatism characteristic of farmers may ave led to rowing rice on
land thet would produce more food from other crops. Indien corn end
Irish potatoes, for example, erow remarkebly well et high elevations in
some localities. Recent studies indicate thet Iriah potatoes in some
locelitics may be rown successfully preceding a crop of rice. Planted
in February, they may be dug in June in amole time for trensplanting
rice. The practice seems especially promising for erees and seasons
where the rice crop matures too late in the fall to permit seeding wheat
or barley at the normal time. However, it is not necessarily limitad to
such situations.
9. Inability to prepare the erouid and serd promptly after
harvestin & crop of rice sometimes reduces yields of the subsequent crops
of wheat and berley. More draft animals or a limited use of labor saving
machinery designed for and adapted to Japanese conditions might result in
material improvements.
10. Losses of food in storege fron insect damage, high moisture
content, and poor storage facilities are not unknown in Japan. Rela-
tively simple and inexpensive studies of such losses, or merely putting
into effect well known and approved prectices could materially increese
the supply of food available for actual consumption.
11. Land reclamatiqn in Japan has for many years been & favorite
subject for speculative discussion, and several attompts have been made at
various times by the central government to add to the area of cultivated
lend. Recent attempts heve not been conspicuously successful 17/.
Another pro ram is under way which contemplates the ultimate reclemation
of approximately 1,550,000 hectares. Many problems are involved to wnich
the experiment stetions could make importent contributions. Some of these
are the fertility ena productivity of the proposed sites, suitable crops
and cultural methods adapted to them, and yields that cen reasonably be

DATE: February 4, 1948.
TO
FROM : NA - R. Fearey
suBJEcT: SFE 185/1, "Policies Concerning Japanese Agriculture".
You will recall that you and Mr. Hamilton asked me to
discuss with General McCoy and Mr. Whitman three alternative
procedures for dealing with the above paper. I wrote the
alternatives down after our meeting, as in Appendix I, and
shortly afterward took them up with General McCoy and
Mr. Whitman, whose reactions are set forth in Appendices II
and III. You will see that both prefer Alternative 3, i.e.
to put a U.S. policy paper such as SFE 185/1 into the FEC.
A further development is the submission of the P&o view
on SFE 185/1 (Appendix IV). An official in the Army Depart-
ment who prefers to remain nameless tells me that it was
written by Colonel Byroade pretty much on his own authority
and was not seen or approved at "higher levels". He (the
above-mentioned ofricial) says that the paper will probably
94.
be withdrawn and a new CAD-P&O paper submitted to SFE. He
feels quite sure, however, that though this new paper will
be milder, the higher levels of the Army Department will not
be willing to approve 185/1.
9
In view of the decision last week to go ahead with the
submission of a revised FEC-230 to the FEC, my personal view
is that we should also go along with the decision to submit
-448
an agrarian reform policy paper. It seems clear, however,
that there will have to be a great deal more discussion of
SFE 185/1 between the State and Army Departments (I hope not
as much as over FEC-230, though it begins to look that way)
before an agreed U.S. position is reached.
I believe Mr. Whitman still hopes for an opportunity
to discuss the matter with Mr. Butterworth as proposed in
his memo of last week.
Perhape waurd
cet
NA:RAF/hhc

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
APPENDIX I
Alternative Procedures Proposed by
Mr. Penfield and Mr. Hamilton
Alternative No. 1
Restate the problem to read something like: to determine
U.S. policy towards FEC O42/1 (agrarian reform paper intro-
duced by the Soviet member). The paper would contain a review
of theSoviet paper and would list the various changes which
would have to be made in it to make it acceptable to the U.S.
Alternative No. 2
To instruct General McCoy to take the position with regard
to the Soviet paper that it was inappropriate for such a paper
to be submitted in view of the fact that General MacArthur had
already carried forward a comprehensive agrarian reform program
under the broad authority and instructions contained in his
basic directives. General McCoy would be asked to point out
that a paper such as that proposed by the Soviet member would
be a purely ex post facto treatment of the problem, and that
the proper thing for the Soviet member or any other member of
the Commission to do if he found fault with the reform program
currently being implemented in Japan would be to call for a re-
view of the SCAP directive of December 9, 1945 to the Japanese
Government, or of some other SCAP action with regard to agrarian
reform, in accordance with paragraph II, A, 2, of the terms of
reference of the Far Eastern Commission as set forth in the
Moscow communiqué.
Alternative No. 2
To adopt SFE 185/1 with certain textual changes in paragraph
6a.

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.99)
APPENDIX II
Mr. Whitman's Comments on Alternatives in
APPENDIX I
Alternative No. 1
There would be three disadvantages to this course:
1. We have repeatedly and definitely promised the other
members of the FEC that we would submit an agrarian reform
paper. The British and Soviets have been reminding us of that
obligation about every month or every two months, though they
have not done so in the last six weeks or so.
2. In specifying the unsatisfactory provisions of the
Soviet paper, we would also have to specify what would be
satisfactory to us, with the result that we would have taken a
position in the matter the same as if we had submitted our own
paper.
3. The danger of the U.S. formally approving a Soviet
agrarian reform paper providing for the far reaching farm re-
forms now being carried out in Japan would be about as great
as for us to initiate a paper of our own specifying these re-
forms.
Alternative No. 2
The disadvantages here are:
1. That we have promised to submit a paper.
2. That we have several times submitted ex post facto
policy papers of our own (for example FEC 230, the arms paper,
the agricultural associations paper, etc.) so we would scarcely
be in a position to say that a Soviet agrarian reform paper at
this stage was inappropriate.
3. We don't want deliberately to invite FEC review of
SCAP actions in the matter if we can help it.
Alternative No. 3
OE strongly prefers this course.

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.99)
APPENDIX III
General McCoy's Comments on Alternatives in
APPENDIX I
Dr. Blakeslee stated that General McCoy's views were as
follows:
1. Inasmuch as the U.S. has repeatedly promised to submit
a farm reform policy paper, this promise should be honored.
Dr. Blakeslee said that General McCoy was disturbed over the
fact that several similar U.S. commitments in the past had not
been fulfilled.
2. It would be inadvisable to encourage members of the
Commission to review acts or programs of the Supreme Commander
in Japan.
3. It is desirable for the U.S. to submit policy papers
on all our more important programs in Japan so that the Com-
mission may have opportunity to approve them and build up a
dossier of internationally agreed occupation policies.
General McCoy accordingly prefers Alternative 3 (to submit
a U.S. policy paper) for much the same reasons as Mr. Whitman.

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.99)
BECRET
ENCLOSURE
POLICIES CONCERNING JAPANESE AGRICULTURE
Memorandum by the P&O Member. SFE
1. Although at the moment, the subject of Japanese Agri-
culture (and Land Reform) is not of direct military concern,
certain long-range U.S. military views now being considered
envision a Japan economically self-supporting and of potential
value as a deterrent to further Soviet expansion in Eastern
Asia. A program of socialization of Japanese agriculture, as
complete as that proposed in this paper, even though already
initiated by the SCAP, would tend to weaken the overall re-
covery of Japan, a recovery which would be essential at an
early date should there be adopted by the U.S. Government the
strategic concept of building Japan, to the extent practicable,
into a bulwark against further Soviet expansion.
2. It 1s agreed that some deconcentration of large Japanese
absentee landed estates is necessary and 1s in line with a decon-
centration of excessive economic power, now being implemented.
However, a recent public announcement of a moderation of the
decentralization of Japanese industry has set a precedent for a
reexamination of the idea of completely socializing Japanese
agriculture.
3. Pending an exhaustive reexamination, on the highest U.S.
political and military levels, of our long-range strategic aims
1n Japan, the U.S. Government should be extremely reluctant to
confirm officially the socialization of Japanese agriculture.
4. Therefore. the P&o Member cannot concur in the Conclus-
1ons and Recommendations expressed in SFE 185/1.

hopes of becoming owmer-cultivators. The majority of the teaests
who have already bought land have made initial peyments smeunting
to as mueh as sirty to seventy peroent of the entire purehase prise,
a fact refleeting the money prosperity of rural commanities. This
phenomemom has mot been so apparent in villages removed frem large
urban or industrial centers where farmers do not have the opportumity
to sell their vegetables and other produee on the blaek markst.
The farm union movement among the farmers in the prefeetures
which Mr. Ladejinsky visited has had much to do with the pregress
of land reform. In Kagawa eighty-one percent of the villeges have
bramches of ome or the other of the two dominant unions, the redieal
Japan Farmers Union (Nichino) and the more conservative Natiomal parm-
ers Union (Zeano). Union influence bas mmde it possible in this pre-
feeture for tenants to preside over forty-four peroent of the leeal
land cemmissions. (Thirty-one peroent are presided over by owmer-
cultivators and twenty-mime pereent by landlords.) The average per-
centage of tenent-obairmem for Japan as a whole is twenty-four per-
comt.
owmer-eultivator

Owmer-cultivator commission members on both the loeal and pre-
fectural levels generally vote with the tenants and have beem in-
strumemtal in persuading landlords to do likewise even on some
rather crucial issues. In another way, however, the owmer-eulti-
vator has mot been helpful. The problem of comsolidatiom of mall,
local holdings has apparently not been affected by the pregress of
the land reform or any other program, Mr. Ladejinsky states. The
owmer-eultivator's land, which is not subject to the previsiems of
the reform, stamds in the way of consolidation, whieh eammet take
place until this olass of farmer is compelled to submit his holdings
to redistribution by land commissions.
Significant im the past year has been the deoline in the land
owmers' oppositiom to the progrem. In the summer of l947 seme land-
lords initiated a number of suits for the purpose of testing the
constitutionality of the land reform law, but most of these eases,
although mo longer pending, have never come to trial. Rather them
test the constitutionality of the reform laws, landlerds in large
numbers are now attempting, it is stated, to gain possession of tem-
anted land in order to raise food for their own consumptiom. The
permission of the prefectural governor is necessary in order to ter-
mimate or reseind the leases given their tenants. The settlements
in many of these cases have not beem strictly in aceord with the
provisions of the law, but Mr. Ladejinsky is not certain that it is
either feasible or advisable to insist on the letter of the law im
every instance. The net results of the legal and illegal evietiems
is the inerease of the so-called "mall consumption farms", whiek are
relatively less productive and do not contribute to the matiomal food
supply. The landlords involved in these eviction cases are, in the
main, small scale resident owners who have found it impossible to
live on the very mall money rentals permitted them by law.
According to the provisions of the law, rents for rice fields
may be as high as twenty-five percent of the value of the crop. How-
ever, with the progress of inflation, rents have become a frestion
of one percent of that value. This fraction is reduced still further
if one considers that the commutation of rent to a money basis, im-
stituted by the Agricultural Land Adjustment Law of December 28, 1945,
deprives the landlord and awards to the tenant the value of the riee
straw, which is a bi-product of the crop. The value of the strew
alone pays, Mr. Ladejinsky states, many times over the rent of the
land. To correct this situation, Mr. Ladejinsky recommends that tem-
ants be required by law to pay a rental bearing a reasonable relatiom-
ship to the use they make of the land. He asserts that they are mow
in a financial position to do this and that the longer the presemt
situation continues, the more difficult it will become for the temants
to realize that renting land at virtually no ∞st to themselves will
not be possible much longer.

One of the important omplaints of the landowmers is that the
twenty-four year annuity bonds, bearing interest at the rate of
3.65%, which they are given in exohange for their land, have beem
decreed by the Ministry of Finance to be non-negotiable withis five
years from the date of issuance. Mr. Ladejinsky believes that this
complaint would be met if landowmers were granted the opportunity to
cash their bonds as ciroumstances dictate.
It was one of Mr. Ladejinaky's observations that the work of the
land commissions which are the administrators and exeeutors of the
land reform program is seriously impaired by lack of funds and com-
sequently by insufficient personnel. On some occasions it has beem
necessary for villagers to contribute voluntarily from their owm fim-
ances to provide a commission with the minimum assistance to permit
its contimued operation.
The eoonomic changes now taking place in rural Japan, Mr. Lade-
jinsky observes, have resulted in the improvement of the lot of the
great majority of the farmers and in the worseming of that of the
landlords. Political end social changes, parallel to the eeomomie
changes, are less obvious. The need for a new rural leedership has
arisen, and the old order can persist in a new guise only in the event
that no suoh new leaderahip emerges. Tenants and ewmer-cultivaters
now have the opportunity, Mr. Ladejinsky declares, and in fast ase
being compelled, to take an active part in the affairs whiek at eme
time were the sole preserve of the landowmers, who with their less
of affluemce have also lost some of their influemee. The farm umiems,
the land commissions, and the agricultural co-operatives will supply
much of the needed talent but not in the very near future. It is
likely that leaderahip in the village in the time immediately akeed
will be shared between the landowners and the other local groups.
Especially interesting, Mr. Ladejinsky comments, is the positiem
of the Communist Party in the Japanese village. He notes that the
party has lost influence because of the failure of its propaganda
against land purohases by tenants. The main line of this attaek was
not against land reform as such, but against the partioular type of
reform now being carried out with its main mphasis on private
rather tham state, individual rather than colleetive owmership ef
land.
Drawing upon his first-hand knowledge of the mistakes of the
Kerensky Govermment at the time of the Russian revolutiom, Mr. Lade-
jinsky evokes a picture of what might have happened in Japan had
land reform never come about, asserting that the country might mow
be a cauldron of unrest and discontent, and fertile grownd for eem-
munist agitation. In this conmestion, he believes tribute should be
paid to the Supreme Commander's thorough appreciation of the lessoms
of histery

of history and their proper application to the specific conditioms
that exist in Japan.
In conclusion, the writer surveys the effeetiveness of the mil-
itary Government Teams which he calls the instrumemts through which
it is hoped to impart new meaning to Japan's economy, polity, and
society. He takes note of the fact that although some members of
the military Government Teams take exception to the underlying prin-
ciples of the land reform program, these differences of opimiom do
not adversely affect the enforoement of the reform, the effort to

of land reform, must operate. More and better personnel, he states,
has been a basio need in the past; in the days ahead, it will be an
absolute necessity.

h. The nature of the probleme encountered by an offieer of

in many communities free sales of fish and vegetables are permitted,

reducing still further the volume entering legal channels.

k. Despite the difficulties encountered in colleetimg this

but note the high degree of the farmers' compliance. The tendemsy
of the peasant the world over, when unable to obtain for his prodace
a price with sufficient purchasing power in terms of capital or
consumers' goods, is either to decrease prodnction, or to censme more
of his produce within his family, or to divert as much as he cam to
the black market, where prioes are much higher than the offieiel retes.
The Japanese farmer did not resort to the first alternative, emsept
only in so far as he attempted to shift some of his acreage to mere
profitable food crops. He resorted to black marketing, and, en oese-
sion, at the expense of his own consumption levels. Generelly speak-

no special reason for liking, and in return he received cheep paper
money. Since the end of hostilities the lot of the farmer has impreved
greatly, while that of the town has worsened measurably. It is imeum-
best upon the former to help the latter. The only question is whether
he can be oompelled to give until it really hurts, thus lending new
meaning to the old feudal saying that "a farmer is like a sesamm plemt;
the more you squeeze the more you get", or whether we should stop mhort
of that, thereby providing him with an incentive to maintain produetion
and continue quota deliveries.

8. Land Reform:
a. The prefectures of Kagawa, Okayama, Fukuoka, Saga and
Kumamoto represent some of the oldest regions of what has been referred
to as Old Japan. This region differs not only from the more resemtly
developed Hokkaido with its ample elbow-room, but from northerm Homshu
as well; it exemplifies to an extreme degree the basic feature of japam's
agricultural economy - too many farmers and too little land. The pre-
feetures visited also have, without exception, too many landlerds and
too many tenants. Less than an acre per tenant family is almost the
rule, and a holding of ten aores rented out to fifteen tenants is a
normal pattern. Eorbitant rentals, reaching as high as 75 pereemt of
the rice crop, were part of the pattern.
b. The fact that these prefectures have so many mall land-
lords has given rise to problems that will be disoussed in subsequemt
paragraphs. In the main they do not affeet the progress of the land
reform. A large part of the land purchase progrm can be considered
accomplished, particularly when one adds the amount of land turmed over
to the gowernment in lieu of taxpayments. The land acquired in the
latter manner ranges from 10 to 25 peroent of the originally estimated
amount subjeet to purohase under the land refom law. Altogether,
the Japanese government has already acquired 86 percent of the pur-
chaseable land in Fukuoka, 97 in Kagawa and Okayama, 99 in Kumemoto,
and 123 percent in Saga. The case of Saga is significant, for this is
one of the prefectures where the domination of the landlords over the
tenants was supposedly impregnable and indisputable. This is perhaps
the very reason why the land commissions are bent on completing the pur-
chases at an early date.
c. The land has been purchased at official prices set for the
respective prefectures. Inquiries elicited no evidence as to mhether
landlords and tenants have been entering into private fimancial arrenge-
ments to bolster up the price of land. My impression is that for the
most part the purchases are bonafide, that, if anything, the tendeney
on the part of the tenants has been to reduee the price of the land to
a minimum. They achieve this by subtracting from the official price of
land the "cultivation right" price paid for by a tenant at ome time or
another. The cultivation right practice is widespread in Kagawa; less
so in Okayama, Kumamoto and Saga, and it exists only im a few gams in
Fukuoke. Where such rights do exist, the official price of land is re-
duced by l0 to 30 percent. In some villages sueh as Kojo village
(Okayama prefecture), which I visited, it accounted for 40 pereemt of
the official price of land. The temants are entitled to these "refunds",
and this is recognized by the landlords. Yet they are umamimous in the
demand
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.97)
Bmol. to Tokyo's
No. B1 dated
May 15, 1948.
CONFIDENTIAL
-7-
demand that they receive the full price of the land as provided
under the law, and that the govermment shoulder the burdem of the
cultivation right payments. In view of the fact that the imflaties-
ary spiral has, to date, reduced materially the official priees fimed
in early l946, it would not be unreasonable for the Japanese soverm-
ment to reviow the "cultivation right" problem im the light of reeemt
developmonts.
d. Sales of land to tenants have begun and will sarely be
completed by the end of the year. I visited Kabe village (Kagawe
prefecture) when the land had already been sold to the tememts. ome
would have to see the remarkable statistical work of the loeal land
commission to appreciate the earnestness emd seal with which it is
carrying out the reform. A glance at the bar graphs, brightly colored,
reveals the amount of land purchased and sold by the village as a mhele
and by buraku, while an excellent map records by mstual measuremems
every plot of land sold to the tenants. It is of interest to mote thet
the moving spirit of reform in this village is a Mr. Oyame, land-
lord-member of the comaission. He not only sold all of his land,
but succeeded in inducing twenty-five other landlords to part with
all of their allowable retention, and ten other landlords to give up
part of their allowable retention. This enabled a mumber of temeats
to acquire land which they could not have bought otherwise. In
Kumamoto some of the villages are setting aside a eertain pereemtage
of the purehased land (usually 10 percent) for such purpose.
e. I have listened to many a tenant expressing comeerm over
the financial risks that go with owmership, but in no case that I
know of was this anxiety strong enough to oweroome the sherished hope
of becoming an owner-cultivator. "A farmer without land," a temamt
remarked, "is like a man without life." The initial paymeats of the
majority of the tenants who have already bought land emount to as mach
as 60-70 pereent of the purchase price of the land, which testifies
to the cash-prosperity of the mural community. Yet the pieture is
spotty. Tenants in certain villages insisted that they canmot make
large initial payments even though fully aware of the advantages of
doing so. These villages are removed from large urban or industrial
centers, and are denied the opportunities of growing and selling vege-
tables or trading other products in the blackmarket. Taxation, whish
they claim is levied on the theory that all farmers engage in bleck-
marketing, and the purchase of fertilizer and other items at black-
