INSECT AND DISEASE CONTROL
The control of insect pests and diseases of plants in Japan probably
received less attention than any other phase of agriculture prfor to 1947.
This was due partially to a lack of information on damage cauled and.
consequently, to lack of appreciation of control importance. Japanese
Government statistics have indicated a probable annual loss to orop pro-
duction of l.5 percent from insects, and a like percentage from plant
diseases. Occupation force agricultural specialists believe these figures
to be unrealistically low. Japanese professional entomologists and plant
pathologists share this view. Estimates of American specialists after.
four years of first-hand observation are that these two sources of loss
reduce crops at least 10 percent annually.
Most of the entomological work in Japan has been in the laboratory.
and some good results have been obtained. However, field work has been
neglected with the result that for a number of pests practical methods
for field control have not been developed. Equipment for the control of
plant pests has been inadequate. Dusts are more easily applied and less
expensive for the control of many insects than are sprays. Prior to 1947
the use of pesticides in the dust form in Japan was practically non-existent.
Sprays were applied to fruit trees from a limited number (less than 5,000
for all Japan) of antiquated power sprayers, or from 2 - 5 gallon kmapsaok
or compressed air sprayers. All treatment of field and vegetable crops was
done by low pressure hand sprayers, in many instances of low efficienoy.
Because equipment was not available, farmers were unable to earry on a
complete pesticide control program. During 194l-45 the supply of many
pesticides, including the arsenates, nicotine sulfate and derris root, was
inadequate. The combination of inadequate equipment and the shortage of
pesticides probably was partially responsible for continued emphasis on
many comparatively ineffective methods of insect control. Such ineffective
methods include catching rice beetles in a wooden box swung by hand over
rice plants and using light traps for rice borer control.
The high cost and inefficiency of the pesticides was another factor
causing continued use of out-of-date methods rather than chemicals. Limited
quantities of manganese and iron(ferric) arsenate were used for insect con-
trol. These arsenicals have been shown to be inferior to the standard lead
and calcium arsenates by extensive tests in other countries, and the cost
is appreciably higher. Low insecticide content, inferior materials, incor-
rect combinations, and recently for dusts, unsatisfactory grinding, were all
causes of unsatisfactory results from insecticides.
The principal insecticides used in Japan prior to World War II, as
indicated by the l936-40 average annual consumption, include lead arsenate
1,726 metric tons, calcium arsenate. 496 metric tons, nicotine sulfate 129

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.98)
metric tons, derris root 140 motrio tons, and pyrethrum flowors 1,585
tons. The use of caloiumi arsenete inoreased rapidly from the time of
introduotion in 1934. By 1941 the amount of caleium arsenate used mae 3
matric tons and lead arsenate used was l,l68 metric tons. The ohange
partially to the replacement of lead, a war metal, with the relatively
and abundant caloium.
Prior to 1947 lead arsenate was used to control inseot pests of fruits
and vegetables. Caloium arsenate was applied primarily to vegetables and
white potatoe's, with a limited quantity to control rice leaf beetles. Nicotine
sulfate was used primarily as a contact insecticide on fruits and vegetables
and to alimited extent on rice ssed beds. Derris root was used to control
chewing insects on fruits and vegetables when arsenate could not be used be-
cause of the danger to humans. Pyrethrum served as a. substitute for nicotind
sulfate and was used extensively in oil emulsions for the control of rioe
leaf hoppers.
During the years 1936-40 Japan produced more arsenates than were oon-
sumed domestically, and annually exported several hundred tons of lead
arsenate to Far Eastern countries. However, insecticides, partioularly the
arsenicals, were in short supply in 1945-47. The complete change of industry
to war production during 1941-45, and the post-war economic situation, with
reduced mining activities, resulted in Japanese production of little arsenious
acid during 1944-46. Most of that produced was used in industry. The supply
of lead arsenate in 1945 was 250 metric tons, about one-eighth of the estimated
need. All arsenic for insecticides was imported in 1946 and some in 1947.
As a result of increased production of arepious acid during the last two years
indigenous production of arsenates met the demand in 1948-49 and 300 metrio
tons were exported during early 1949.
Insecticide and fungicide supplies generally. have been adequate to meet
farmers' demands during the current year. In the first half of l949 lead
arsenate distributed to farmers was 1,214 metric tons, more than half the
amount used in 1948. During the first half of 1949 consumers also had 805
metrio tons of calcium arsenate, 120 tons less than the total used in 1948
and more than 300 tons above the 1936-40 annual average usage. No derris
root and little nicotine sulfate are produced in Japan. Significant quanti-
ties of thase insecticides were not available during 1946-48. In early 1949
nicotine sulfate, 144 metric tons, and derrie root, 193 metric tons were
imported and released to farmers in late June and July.
During 1936-40 pyrethrum was produced and used extensively in Japan and
was an export item.  The use of pyrethrum as an agrioultural pesticide dropped
from 1,590 metric:tons annually during 1936-40 to 805 metric tons in 1948,
and a first six-months' 1949 sale of 545 metric tons. This reduotion is due
parttally to reduced stocks of pyrethrum resulting from a amaller acreage
planted: Increased use of synthetio organic insecticides, particularly DDT
and BHC (benzene hexachIoride) has reduced the demand for pyrethrum.
The use of DDT and BHC, from imports and indigonous production, reduced
the demand for other insecticides to some extent. DDT, initially for health
and welfare purposes, was first manufactured in Japan in 1946. A amall

Other promising new insecticides, including chlordane, toxaphene,
and parathion, have been imported for testing in Japan. Courteay donations
of the manufacturers in the United States have been responsible for such
imports.
Although the supply of fungicides gonerally has been adequate in: recent
years, serious plant disease epidemics have resulted from insuffioient edu-
cation of farmers in the importance of timely use of fungicides. As an ex-
ample; improper and/or inadequate spray ing of white potatoes in 1948 and 1949
resulted in a heavy loss of that crop through late blight. Educational
programs on plant disease control are receiving continuing emphasis from
Natural Resources Section personnel.
Although insecticide and fungicide stocks are generally adequate to meet
current demands of farmers on a national basis, reserve stocks of many
pesticides are small. Allocations of some pesticides still are made to con-
suming areas on the basis of stocks available, and local emergenoy demands
at times result in shortages. Such problems arise from lack of reserve stooks
and problems of distribution that cannot be foreseen. A slight increass in -
reserve stocks would alleviate the situation. Indications from research now
in progress on insect control are that demands for insecticides in the future
will be increased substantially above present levels.
In addition to improvements in the supply and types of pesticides,
Occupation officials are working toward the improvement of control practices.
A large acreage of rice in north Honshu and Hokkaido is seriously damaged
each year by rice leaf miners. Little has been done in the past to control
this insect with pesticides. It is considered highly probabie that timely
applications of synthetic organic insecticides will do the job.
Rice borers are considered generally to cause more damage than any other
insect in Japan. Research work is in progress to develop positive control
mearures. In 1946-49 over one billion yen were spent on the installation
3

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.99)
of blue fluoresdent light traps. This mathod of control has
for many insects in the United States, including those simila
borers, and has been discarded in that country as ineffective.
ducted in Japan indicate that light trape are at best medioare

rice growing areas of Japan, might increase rice production by as mumh

Some improved methods of preventing insect damages would be impossible
under methods of insecticide application used in Japan prior to 19e7. In
June 1947 Japanese engineers were furnished information on the prineiple
and use of insecticide dusts and aerosols (fog), and on the prinoiples of
construction of machines for dispensing insecticides in these forms. One
Japanese company has designed and manufactured a hand rotary duster anda
power dusterfog generator. By 30 April 1949 this company had sold 40,605
durable, aluminum hand rotary dusters, and l67 power-operated dusterfog
machines. With the hand duster a farmer easily is able to treat his entire
farm in one day, and the pcwer machine oan be used cooperatively to apply
pesticides to crops in entire villages.
The low quality of pesticides has been a oritical problem in Japan.
Prior to l948 specific labeling of pesticides showing details of composition,
usage, and effectiveness was not reguired. At the instigation of agricultural
specialists of Natural Resourcos Section, an Agricultural Chemical Control
Law was promulgated in 1948 to alleviate that situation. An extensive edu-
cational campaign is planned to stimulate public opinion for enforoement of
this law. A complete Plant Quarantine and Emergency Pest Control Bill has
been prepared, with assistance and encouragement of Natural Resources Section
personnel. It is expected that the bill will be presented to the next
session of the Diet.

THE SEED SITU.TION IN J:P.N
Agriculturists know that remarkable difforences in yields are obtained
from dirferent verieties of the same crop growm under comparable conditions
For centuries in Japan, as in other countries, it was assumed that elr e

LIVESTOCK·IN JAPAN
The livestock industry in Japan is small and of minor importaboe
as a food source when compared to crop production. Animal foods, other
than fish, have never. been an important part of the Japanese diet.
Prior to the Meiji Restoration in 1868 the Japanese people consumed ml-
most no meat or milk. Since that time they have used increased amounts
of livestock products for food. However, prior to l94l animaI foods
other than fish amounted to only a little more. than one:peroent of the
total food consumed. Even-this meager amount decreased during and after
World War II because of the reductions in the meat animal population:
During 1931-40 about 1,000,000 metric tons of concentrate feeds
were 'imported annually into Japan. Since World War II feed imports have
been negligible in comparison to the prewar volume.: Lack of concentrate
feeds caused an especially large reduction.in numbers of:peultry,:swine,
dairy cows, and rabbits. Populations of those anfmals. aubsisting prima-
rily on roughage were not so adversely affected.
Livestock, although relatively faw in number when compared to the:
United States, play an important role in the Japanese agricultural eoono-
my. .Horses and draft cattle are necessary to provide draft power on
farms and for city transportation. Present populatiops of horses and
'draft cattle are considered sufficient to supply draft power peeds for
the'14;900,000 acres of land now in cultivation if maximum usage is
obtained from these animals. However, small farms, many less than't
aore in size, and inequities in distribution of draft animals will
continue to require that some farms operate without draft power:" Land
reclamation may be expected to. increase the need for draft animals to
a limited extent.
Increased numbers of dairy cows, dairy goats, and sheep are de-
sirable. Milk is needed in larger quantities to supply even the minimum
needs for infants, invalids, and pregnant women. Significant inorease
in population of dairy.cows in Japan appeas feasible in Hokkaido.and
northern Honshu. Hokkaido particularly appears to have a noteworthy
potential for enlargement of its dairy industry. Climatio conditions
are satisfactory for dairy purposes and relatively unfavorable for many
agricultural crops. In addition, the area. is relatively sparsely popu-
lated when compared to the .remaining part of Japan; and farms averago:
several times larger than those in the other islands. Larger farms

and for diversified farming operations essential to maximum food.output
on the island. Milk goats are being encouraged for areas where pasture

During 1931-40 about 1,000,000 metric tons of concentrate feeds
were 'imported annually into Japan. Since World War II feed imports have
been negligible in comparison to the prewar volume. Lack of concentrate
feeds caused an especially large reduction.in numbers of peultry,:swine,
dairy cows, and rabbits. Populations of those animals. aubsisting prima-
rily on roughage were not so adversely affected.

farms and for city transportation. Present populatiops of horses and
'draft cattle are considered sufficient to supply draft power needs for
the'14;900,000 acres of land now in cultivation if maximum usage is
obtained from these animals. However, small farms, many less than't
acre in size, and inequities in distribution of draft animals will
continue to require that some farms operate without draft power: Land
reclamation may be expected to. increase the need for draft animals to
a limited extent.
Increased numbers of dairy cows, dairy goats, and sheep are de-
sirable. Milk is needed in larger quantities to supply even the minimum
needs for infants, invalids, and pregnant women. Significant increase
in population of dairy.cows in Japan appears feasible in Hokkaido.and
northern Honshu. Hokkaido particularly appears to have a noteworthy
potential for enlargement of its dairy industry. Climatio conditions
are satisfactory for dairy purposes and relatively unfavorable for many
agricultural crops. In addition, the area. is relatively sparsely popu-
lated when compared to the .remaining part of Japan; and farms average
several times larger than those in the other islands. Larger farms
provide good potential for roughage and concentrate feed production,
and for diversified farming operations essential to maximum food.output
on the island. Milk goats are being encouraged for areas where pasture
or feed supplies are inadequate to maintain dairy cattle. Sheep are

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
grown. in Japan chiefly for their wool which is ussd primarily for
spuns for home consumption. Sheep usually are maintained in mmall
flooks of 2 - 5 per farm.
Swine numbers have increased markedly in recent years despite the
shortage of concentrate feeds. Decayed and partially decayed sweet
potatoes, other perishables, and feeds not suited for human consumption,
are the principal sources of swine feed.
Poultry decreased approximately 70 percent during 1941-45 because
of the shortage of feed. Only a slight increase has been achieved to
date, largely in small farm flocks.
The livestock industry prior to the Oocupation was bighly organised
and controlled by the Government. Several livestock control companies,
operated for the benefit of a few men in control. supervised the procure-
ment and distribution of livestock and livestock products. These control
companies have been dissolyed as a result of directives from SCAP.
The Government maintains Livestock Breeding Stations to provide
breeding services for farmers. Emphasis in the past was given almost
entirely to horses, largely for military purposes. Currently horses
receive most support, but some attention is given to dairy cattlo and
other classes of livestock. The need for these breeding stations has
not been demonstrated. Observations of Agrioulture Division personnel
have shown that farmer breeders are capable of breeding and growing
animals of equal or superior quality to those produced on livestock
breeding stations. Many breeders of dairy cattle, draft cattle, swine,
horses, and chickens have demonstrated this ability beyond question.
The Livestock Bureau, MAF, currently is engaged in a reduction program
relative to national livestock breeding stations. As a result of recem-
mendations by NR, in 1949 seven national livestock breeding farms of a
total of 25 were eliminated. Others were reduced in size. The Ministry
expects to discontinue some other breeding farms in 1950.
Natural Resources Seetion has established certain policies directed
toward the establishment of an economically sound livestock program for
Japan. Basic tenets of this program are:
a. The program must be based on private ownership of animals.
Private ownership includes ownership by agricultural cooperatives.
b. Sufficient numbers of draft horses and/or draft cattle must
be maintafned to insure maximum food production, facilitate transporta-
tion of material, and alleviate, to the extent feasible, the labor burden.
of the Japanese farn family.
c. Efficient and maximum utilization must be made of draft horses
and cattle.

forago plants proved by test to be more produetive than tpes

g. Production of dairy goats, because of their small sise and

means of improving the nutrition and income of the Japanese farm family.
h. Production of sheep, rabbits, and poultry should be oncoutaged.

i. Government aid in the form of improved researoh and educatlonal

FOOD STORAGE AND PRESERVATION IN JAPAN
Storage and preservation of food in Japan is diffioult. The
humid summers throughout most of the country cause stored grein
In addition, the rainy weather usually prevailing in the late sprimg
and fall harvest seasons causes farmers great difriculty in adequataly
drying their grain crops before moving them into market channels. The
approximately 6,000,000 metric tons of sweet potatoes harvested during
the period 20 September - 1 December each year present the major food
storage problem. Consumers will not accept the quantity of potatoes
farmers seek to market during this period and inadequate facilities pre-
vent farm or commercial storage through the winter. Freesing weather
in most areas prevents shipment and handling of potatoes.betiween
10 December - 1 March.
Japan has had adequate capacity to store its grain orops for
number of years. Most warehouses were improved prior to 1940.and are.
of substantial construction. However, few are rodent proof and none
air conditioned. Because of these deficiencies, it will be difricult
to stockpile grains and to make larger purchases of food in periods of
abundant supply and lower prices.
Prior to 1940 sweet potato production approximated 3,000.,000
metrio tons annually, or about 50 percent of current production. Most
of the crop was consumed soon after harvests, and the storage problem
was minimized. However, many farmers stored substantial quantities,of
sweet potatoes in caves. The success of such storage varies greatly.
but in many instances spoilage has caused high losses.
For many years vegetables have been preserved successfully by
salting, pickling, or drying. However, the absence of adequate re-
frigeration and shipping facilities historically has cuased major
losses in these products during movement from producing to consuming
areas.
The Agriculture Division of Natural Resources Section has re-
cognized since the beginning of the Occupation the necessity for an
adequate food storage program, particularly for sweet potatoes. The
need includes also air conditioned grain storages to permit larger
purchases at lower prices on world markets during food surplus periods,
and limited procurement during years of poor crops in supplier coun-
tries. Definito action to implement a grain storage program has been
delayed by the post-war shortage of building materials and by the
financial straits of the Japanese Government.

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.98)
Inoreased weet potatt prbdustion from the l9s1-40 annuel avera
of 3,000,000 metric tonè to the current level of about 6,000,000 metrie
tohs has created a ma jor utilization and storage problem. The 3,600,000
metrid tons of sweet potatoes whieh moved into:commoroial channels in
1948 were sufficient to provide the stapte food oomponent for the rationed
population for about 45 days. However, potatees ip quanbity are available
during about 90 days of the year and the Japanese people will not willing
aacept'potatoes during the harvest period. to :the extent of 50 pdroent of

the percentage of potatoes included in the ration was unreasonably high.
Since 1946 Natural Resources Seotion agrioultural specialists in
cooperation with the Ministry of Agrioulture and Forestry have studied
the sweet potato storage problem. Production. of. this crop must be main-
tained at high levels because sweet potatoes produce from two to.three
times the amount of calories per unit area of:land as do competing crops.
During:1331-40 sweet potatoes produced 3.6 metric tons per heotare in'
brown rioe equivalents compared to l.5 metric tons for corn. Ocoupetion
agricultural specialists and Japanese Government officials believe that
consumers could utilize the sweet potato crop effectively if the period
of use for food were extended from three to six or eight months.
The problem of potato storage on farms has received increasing
emphasis since 1945. Japane'se experiment station personnel. for several
years have studied the possibilities of undergroupd storage. The use
of thermostatic controls for such storage to maintain the proper temper-
ature, with suitable ventilation, has been developed to permit overwinter
storage of small lots without excessive lossess  To encourage more ex-
tensive use of farm atorage the Japanese Government has: instituted.a
pricing program under which it will pay 70 percent more for farm-stored
potatoes after 1 March 1950 than the price during the peak harvest season
in the fa11 'of 1949.
In the spring of 1948 at the instigation of the Agriculture Division
of Natural Resources Section, the Japanese Government-submitted a five-
year plan for construction of sweet potato. storage and curing houses
which would provide 110,O00 metric tons additional storage space each
year thrbughout the five-year period. The program has been initiated and
currently 215 buildings are under construotion. It: is expected that those
building will be completed in time to cure and store about 25,000 metrio
tons of the 1949" potato crop.  Implementation of the Japanese Government
five-year plab will alleviate the atorage problem but will:not provide
a complete solution. Storage for at least 1,000,000 metric tons must
eventually be provided if the Japanese people are efficiently to utilize
the sweet potato crop.

SOIL SURVEY IN JAPAN
Basic information on the nature of the soils, their potentiali-
ties and their limitations is necessary to eveluate Japanese agri-
culture properly, and to develop programs for inoreasing crop produe-
tion. Suitable information has been limited because of defioiencies
in Japanese soil surveys which included differences in soil survey
methods and nomenclature used by Japanese solentists and by solentists
in the United States and Eurcpe. A reconnalssance soil survey of Japan
was initiated by the Natural Resources Section in the fall of 1946 to
classify the soils in a more usable form than that employed by the
Japanese.
The first soil maps of Japan were made by the Agronomy Division of
the Imperial Geological Survey under a program instituted in I8a2. Maps
of about two-thirds of the cultivated land had been completed by 1905,
when the work was transferred to the Central Agricultural Experiment
Station. By 1945 all the cultivated land in Japan except parts of
Hokkaido had been surveyed and maps of the surveyed areas had been
published.
The soils classifioation system used by Japanese scientists was
based on texture of the surface soil, geology of the underlying material
and topography of the area surveyed. Soil units were defined in terms
of the kind and geologic age of underlying material, but texture and
humus content of the surface soil and texture of the subsurface layers
were observed and notod in the course of the survey. Published maps
were on a scale of 1: 200,000; cach map or set of maps covered one
prefecture. The kind and age of underlying material were shown by
colors, and texture of the surfaco soil by varicus types of cross-
hatching. Diagrams showing the mechanical composition of the surface
and subsoil layers of the various soil units were printed along the
margins of the maps. The maps were accompanied by brief roports des-
cribing the area surveyed, discussing the agricultural land as to ge-
ological crigin, and attempting to correlate soil fertility and plant
growth with geological formations. Non-agricultural areas were clas-
sified very broadly on the basis of geology, relief and climate. Com-
parable terminology and classification were not used in the varicus
prefectures, consequently the usefulness of the surveys was limited.
About 1936, Japanese soil scientists, who had become increasingly
aware of the inadequacies of their soil surveys, undertook a study of
Russian, German, and American soil classification methods and began to
develop a more scientific scheme of classification. For the first time
climate, biological activity, relief, and age were considered, in addition

=== Page 281 === (confidence 0.99)
to parent material, as important to soil forming processes
y 1/ of the soil profile was amploysd as the basis of clessifioat
Maps of only a few mall areas were completed under this soheme of
classification.
The reconnaissance soil survey of Japan made by American soil
scientists follows the internationally-known system of olassifying solle
by the morphological characteristics of the soil profile. The survey
classes the soils into associations without designating soil series or
delineating soil types. A soil association is a group of sofls with or
without common characteristics geographically associated in an individual
pattern. Each association mapped is designated by the great. sofl group
to which the dominant soil of the association belongs. The associations
are further subdivided by slope groups, drainage conditions and texture
of the surface soils. Persons familiar with soil classification cad
readily grasp the relaticnship between the soils inoluded within any
great soil group:
For convenience in conducting the reconnaissance soil suryey and
in mapping the distribution of the soils, Japan was divided into eight
parts. Field work on all areas has been completed, one report is
published and the seven remaining reports are in the process of publisa-
tion. These reports discuss the relationship of soils to agrfsulturs
and contain basic information which can be used for several purposes,
including:
a. Broad scale planning of programs related to agriculture and
forestry.
b. Application of experimental data on soils in a geperal manner
over wide regions.
c. Selecting areas for more detailed surveys and other studies.
d. Comparing soils of Japan with those of other parts of the
world for which similar studies are available in orderto make practical
use of information from non-Japanese sources on land use and'orop pro-
duction, and to contribute information from Japan that can be applied to
soils of other parts of the world.
e. Locating and planning the construction of highways, airfields
and other engineering projects involving use of soil as a building
material.
Some Japanese soil scientists are now using the system 'of soil
classification used in the Natural Resources Section reconnaissance
survey. Under the guidance of American" soil scientiats, they aro mapp-
ing small areas of forest and grassland that have possibilities of being
developed into cultivated fields.
1 Morphology, soil - The physical constitution of the soil including
the texture, structure, porsity, consistence and color of the various
soil horizons, their thickness, and their arrangement in the soil
profile.

FOOD COLLECTION IN. JAPAN
Japan has been a deficit food producing nation for many years,
but until the World War II period a subatantial leml of imports enmbled
the government to avoid direct control of supply and distribution.
The first governmental program for direct collection of food from farmers
was begun in l940. Institution of this control then did not pecessar1ly
mean that the food situation was worse, but reflected the general
tightening of economic and other restrictions by the government to place
the country on a war footing. Only rice was collected in the first
year. In l94l, wheat and the barleys, were added to the program. As
military demands increased and the maladjustment of supply and demand
was accelerated the government broadened the collection program in
1942 to include the other major staple crops, sweet potatoes and white
potatoes. A rigid colleotion system embracing these crops was maintained
throughout the war. At the end of the war. food imports from the former
overseas possessions and Manchuria were cut off.: A critical food shortage
existed and Japan became heavily dependent on food provided by the ooou-
pying powers to maintain a minimum level of sustenance. Under these
conditions it was necessary to. collect the maximum amount of indigenous
food, and the government under SCAP direction has continued and strengthened
the program of staple food collection.
The staple crop collection. program in Japan is intended to make
availablo for rationing to non-farm families, at controlled prices,
staple food in excess of the farmers' minimum needs for food, seed, and
feed. The magnitude of this program is indicated by the faot that about
80 percent of all staple crops (rice, wheat, barley, naked barley, white
potatoes; sweet potatoes, and some minor cereals and pulses) is subfect
to collection. The remainder is produced by farmers who grow no more
than enough food to maintain their families. About 45,700,000 people.
or 56 percent of the population, are carried on the general consumer
ration roster throughout the year, and an add1tionaI 10,800,000 persons
for a part of the year.
The first'step in the collection process is the determination and
assignment of delivery quotas. The Ministry of Agrioulture and Forestry
determines these quotas for each staple crop before planting time. The
preplanting quotas are based on "normal" production, which takes into
consideration the anticipated area to be planted, average yields from
previous years, and the expected supply of producer goods. Quotas also
reflect anticipated changes in rural population as welf as in livestook
feed requirements. Natural Resources Section specialists review criti-
cally all collection quotas to assure that they are established at real-
istic levels. After approval of the national quota by Natural Resources
Section, delivery quotas are assigned by the Central Government to each

=== Page 283 === (confidence 0.99)
prefecture. The latter sub-allots the qugtes to counties (a
cities. The county chief, an appointed official of the pr
government, further sub-allots the quotas to towns and villege
each lewel the allocation is made with the advice of an Agrioulture
Adjustment Committee, composed of eleoted farmer representativess
the production and delivery of food in quantities above the preplanting
quota level, the government provides a money and inoentive promium.
The preplanting quotas are subject to revision downmward at the
time of harvest on the basis of production estimates. Revision of the
quotas is closely supervised by Natural Resources Section personnel mnd
is followed by a repetition of the sub-allocation process. In December
1948, GHQ, SCAP directed the Japanese Government to take necessary legal
steps to provide that quota revision may be either upward or dowward.
The law now prohibits upward revision of preplanting quotas. This
directive has not been implemented, but action on it is expected during
the 1949 fall session of the Diet.
Staple foods are.purchased at prices calculated by parity formulas.
To maximize food collections, rural information programs stressing food
production and collection, and the provision of selective monetary and
commodity premiums for deliveries above preplanting quotas to provide
economic incentives for production and for timely deliveries,.are used.
An Occupation-sponsored policy has been to encourage the use of
vital consumer items in short supply as a means of stimulating crop
deliveries. Under this incentive goods system farmers mey purchase
at official prices designated quantities of such items as cotton cloth
and rubber shoes upon the delivery of specified quantities of crops.
Efforts are being made to improve the goods distribution system to
make it more effective as a crop delivery stimulant. Progress was
evident in the summer of l94s when changes were made to speed up distri-
bution and allow the farmer more freedom in selection of goods.
Prefectural and local governments are responsible for planning
and executing crop collection programs in their areas to assure ful-
filment of quotas. Assigned quotas are legally collectiblo and failure
to comply may subject a farmer to prosecution. Local authorities gener-
ally do not rely on legal compulsion, however. They employ such devices
as intensive publicity campaigns stressing the need for deliveries, and
commendations and awards to cooperating farmers.
A major problem is the adequacy of the production estimates, which
are the bases for collection quotas. These estimates are being improved
constantly by the Crop Reporting Service of the Ministry of Agriculture
and Forestry. Development of this recently federalized agenoy has been
greatly stimulated since mid-1948 by the permanent assignment to the
Agriculture Division of an experienced crop reporting specialist from
the United States Department of Agriculture.

That equitable sub-allocation of quotas will continue to be difficult

Another type of problem, depending upon education and the passage of
time for solution, is a consequence of decentralized government. . Degentral-

thought at the several levels of government as to quotss and related matters.
To effeot centralised control over the admiuistration of food orop colleptions
under these conditions is difficult, even though the legal basis for..such
control is adequate in theory.
An additional problem relates to implementation of the qurrent economio
stabilization program. In tightening controls on an economy, it is inevi-
table that control programs become somewhat competitive as locally adminis-
tered.
The effoct of less Occupation contral on the collection effort through
dissolution of the prefectural Civil Affafrs teams, scheduled to take place
by 1 November. 1949, remains to be seen. These teams have had a strong
influence on successful collections. Regional teams will assume some of the
surveillance duties formerly exerctsed by the prefectural teams, but Japanese
officials must assume more responsibility than in the past.

In comparing collection by years several factors should be copsidered.
During the principal war years, 1942-44, farmers were subjected to patriotio
and forcible pressure to deliver maximum amounts of food. At the same time
the farm population was relatively low because of departures for military
and war-industry service; the farm population in 1944 was about seven
million, or 20 percent, less than the estimated population in 1948. Low
collection in l945 was due to poor rice and potato crops and the general
economic and political dislocation attending the surrender. Sinoe 1945
production has been steadily increasing. The quantity of food retained
by farmers has likewise been increasing with the farm population.
Comparison of the two most recent crop years is of interest for
current trends. Total collections in 1948 were up sharply from 1947.
As of 31 August 1949 (with deliveries virtually complete) collection of
the crops harvested in 1948 had reached 7,198,000 metrio tons in brown rice
equivalents. This was 14 percent above 1947 and 10 percent below the 1944
wartime peak.
In terms of collection quotas, 110 percent of the assigned 1948 quota
had been delivered. Final deliveries of the l947 crops reached l02 percent
of the assigned quota. The 1948 quota was five percent higher than the 1947
quota.
Deliveries of each of the staple crops in l948 exceeded those of 1947.
Rice collection (including substitutes) was six percent higher in 1948
than in 1947, wheat and barley (including substitutes) 20 percent, aweet
potatoes 58 percent, and white potatoes 28 percent.
The outlook for collection of 1949 crops is encouraging. Deliveries
of summer-harvested crops now in progress are ahead of 1948. As of.
31 August 1949 a total of 1,123,000 metric tons (brown rice equivalents)
of wheat and barley and whito potatoes had been collected, compared with
956,000 tons on the same date last year. This is an increase of 17 percent.
Production of fall crops has not been officially forecast as yet, so that
collection prospects are indefinite. Indications are that the rice crop,
while suffering spotty damage from disease and drought, will exceed the
1948 level. Sweet potatoes may equal or even surpass the record production
of last year. Especially adverse weather conditions between now and harvest
time could, of course, alter the fall crop production outlook. On present
indications, however, the final quotas for both crops are expected to ap-

Maximum staple crop collection in a controlled economy obvioumly s
the fundamental element in food managoment where indigenous supplies
insufficient to meet the subsistence requirements of the population.
With an ineffective collection program the following major developmonte
would be likely:
The rationing of staple food would break down. The coonomic pressure
on farmers and the spread between official prices and black-market prices
are too great to justify an assumption that staple foods would be delivered
to official channels without existence of a rigid collection program
underwritten by law.
Price controls would not function in the absence of adequate distri-
bution and rationing controls.
Effective consumer prices for staple foods would increase substantially.
as evidenced by past experience in Japan whenever the quantity of rationed
food was diminished.
Rosulting pressure for higher wages for urban workers would accentuate
a wage-price inflationary spiral.
A disproportionate distribution of staple foods among the various
income groups would occur. The lowest inoome groups probably would be
unable to purchase enough food to meet minimum requirements.
Probably at this point emergency requisitions of food upon the United
States would be necessary in order to forestall "disease and unrest"
conditions.
Governmental collection and distribution of staple foods apparently
will be necessary for a number of years to come.The basic condition
under which these controls could be terminated would be possession of a
food supply sufficient to meet the consumption demands of the population
at reasonable prices. Such a supply must be obtained through indigenous
production plus imports that Japan is able to pay for through her own
resources. Production is oxpocted to increase by a relatively small
amount, but not enough to substantially affect the food deficit: probably
not enough, over a period of years, to keep pace with the growing popu-
lation. As the country's industrial and export situation improves more
imports can be financed. However, the use of available exchange for purohase
of food and other consumer gocds should be kept at a minimum. In the Im-
mediate future producer goods shculd recoive import preferance to permit
the fullest measure of industrial recovery on which stabilization of the eco-
nomy will largeIy depend. Certainly as long as food imports are financed by
the United States the indigenous food supply must be utilized with maximum
efficiency. That, in effect, means a continuation of the official collection
and distribution system. Changes in detail may be made, but food collection
probably will continue to be neceasary.

JAPANESE LAND REFORM PROGRAM
Abolition of the pernicious land tenure system which has long blighted
Japanese agriculture and impaired development of a peaceful national
economy has been a basic objective of the Occupation. Accomplishment of
this objective is essential to the economic and political well-being of
Japan.
Having an area of 142,270 square miles, Japan is smaller than the
State of California. Only l6 percent of the total area is arable.
Approximately 47 percent of the total population of about B1,800,000
depends on agriculture for all or part of its fncome. Before the sur-
render, about 70 percent of the farmers rented all or part of the land
they cultivated. Only 9 percent of farm households cultivated more than
4.9 acres of land, and 67 percent cultivated less than 2.4 acras. This
overcrowding had led to a highly inequitable land tenure system with
rents ranging from 50 to 70 percent of the annual crop and had resulted
in widespread economic instability and social strife. Repulsed in their
demands for remedial legislation, farmers, during the decade preceding
World War II, consciously and unconsciously gave strong support to the
military group which held out the promise of new lands and prosperity to
be won through aggressive war. This course was thwarted by defeat and
surrender. The tenant class in occupied Japan, with demands for relief
from their intolerable situation still foremost in their minds, remained
a fertile field for any other extremist ideology promising a solution to
their problems.
The elimination of this malignance was recognized as vital to the
promotion of maximum production of staple foods and requisite social
and economic stability. On 9 December 1945, the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers issued a directive to the Japanese Government "to take
measures to insure that those who till the soil of Japan shall have a
more equal opportunity to enjoy the fruits of their labor."
Japanese land reform is based on legislation enacted by the Diet
and promulgated 21 October 1946. It has two main objectives: (1)
transfer of land ownership to farmers who actually till the soil and
(2) improvement of farm tenancy practices for those who continue as
tenants. The program has emphasized private rather than state, and indi-
vidual rather than collective ownership of the land.
The program in each village is administered by a local agricultural
land commission, elected by and from the farmers. The ratio of tenants,
owner-cultivators, and owners on each commission was originally estab-
lished by law at 5, 2, and 3 respectively. With virtual completion of

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.99)
the land transfer phase of the reform and the rosulting inorease in
the pumber of owner oultivators, it was necessary to change repremwn
tation on these commissions prior to the second general election hold
on 18 August 1949. The new ratio is 2 tenants, 2 landowmers and 6
owner-cultivators. Each category elects its own representatives to the
commission. Elected prefectural agricultural land commissions coordinate
and supervise the work of local commissions under the general supervision
of the Central Agricultural Land Commission (National), of which the
Minister of Agriculture and Forestry is chairman.
Agricultural lands subject to purchase have been: (1) lands owned
by absentee landlords; (2) tenant-operated lands in excess of speciried
retention rates; (3) owner-operated lands in excess of retention rates
fixed by law, except those that can be reasonably operated by the farmer
and his immediate family; (4) corporation-owned lands that do not relate
directly to the principal objective of the corporation; and (5) lands
capable of reclamation for agricultural use.
Lands subject to transfer have been purchased by the government
(through the agency of the land commission) for resale to eligible
buyers. Tenants on the land as of 23 November 1945, other tenants, and
other persons who wish to cultivate the land have had priority to pur-
chase. in that order.
The purchase price of land, established by law, is subject to vari-
ation within legal limits at the discretion of the land commisslon.
Sellers of land receive payment in ¥1,O00 bonds bearing 3.65 percent
interest and redeemable in 24 equal annual installments. Any amount less
than a unit of ¥l,Oo0 Is paid in cash. Tenants may pay for all or any
part of the land at the time of purchase and the remainder in 24 annual
installments with interest at 3.2 percent. Tenant payments for the tand,
plus other expenses coincident with landownership, in any. year cannot
exceed one-third of the gross income from the land in that year. In case
of crop failure or low farm prices, the government will reduce, defer or
cancel the annual payment.
Agricultural lands purchased under the program may not be sold or
used as collateral for loans during the 24-year amortization period
without specific approval of the government. Tenant purchesers desiring
to discontinue thoir purchase contract during the 24-year amortization
period must sell their lands back to the government on terms similar to.
those of the original contract. All future land transactions are subject
to approval by the agricultural land commission.
Through the land reform program, the Japanese Goverument had ac-
quired 4.594. 721 acres of cultivated land by 2 July 1949, of which
4,461,688 acres had been sold by 30 June 1949. The cultivated land area
operated by tenants will have thus been reduced from an estimated
6,300,000 acres (46 percent) to approximately 1,670,000 acres (12 percent).
As of the same dates 590,549 acres of pasture land had been acquired, of
2

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.99)
which 418,984 acres had been sold. An estimated additiona1
acres of pasture land await purchase. In addition to curvivae
pasture land, 2,775,488 acres of land has been acquired rer reela
and future resale to owmer-farmers as part or a Tong-range progh
increasing the total cultivated area of the country. of this me
245,l27 acres had been reclaimed and sold to individuals for private
reclamation by 2 July 1949. Registration in the official land regiotry
book of lands transferred under the program is now being aceomplished.
Although the land reform program is dedicated primarily to creating
owner-farmers, total elimination of farm tenancy is pot considered fea-
sible. A small reservoir of land subject to tenancy is considered es-
sential to a program of maximum food production. Flexibility in the
land tenure pattern must be preserved to prevent withdrawal of land
from cultivation in cases where the owner becomes temporarily unable to
cultivate for himself, or where no applicant to purchase land is availa-
ble. The land reform program therefore also includes provisions to in-
sure fair tenancy practices for those who remain as tenants. The most
important of these provision are: (1) a written farm lease with all
principal elements clearly stated; (2) cash payment of rent:; (s) ront
ceiling of 25 percent of the production from paddy and 15 percent from
upland fields; and (4) rent contract cancellation or alteration subjeot
to approval by the land commission. A model lease contraot form to be
used by tenants and landlords as a guide in drawing up their own con-
tracts has been widely distributed.
The total cost to the Japanese Government of administering the land
reform program was ¥7,825,284,203 for the period 1 September 1946 through
30 April 1949. Of this amount ¥6.257.126.605 was spent for the support
of the 11,322 local land commissions. Principal responsibility and
crodit for the success of this program, involving transfer of title to
about 30,000,000 individual parcels of land, accrues to these local land
commissions. The average cost of operating an individual local land com
mission has been approximately 1,535 (computed at an assumed rate of
¥360 to $1) for the period September 1946 to 30 April 1949, or about $48
per month per commission.

AGRICULTURAL COOPERATIVE PROGRAM IN JAPAN
Approximately 5,910,000 farmers are engaged in farming-Japan
14,900,000 aares of cultivated land. Average farmers operate only about
2 acres. The extreme small size of their individual operations makes
It essential that they pool their efforts and resources through cooper-
ation to the greatest practical extent in order to achieve esonomio
stability and maximum production. Towards this end, legislation which
became effective on 15 December 1947, provided for the establishment of
democratic, farmer-controlled cooperative associations in accord with
internationally recognized cooperative principles.
Cooperative associations replace a monopolistic, government-controlled
system of agricultural associations which had been developing in Japan
since l899. Legislation enacted that year established associations to
improve farming practices. Membership was compulsory. A year Tater,
industrial cooperatives were similarly authorized to facilitate trade
and essential business -- especially in rural areas. Although this law
provided for voluntary membership and permitted some degree or independent
action, the Japanese agricultural cooperative movement was from Its
inception primarily a government-sponsored movement. In most instances
the cooperatives were controlled by the local agricultural associatlons
which were in turn agents of the original legislation, especially between
1930 and 1943, and culminating in the Agricultural Organization Law of
1943, steadily increased government control over these organizations,
particularly in relation to farm prices und thereby the entire farm
economy. The 1943 legislation eliminated oven the fiction of democracy,
and the agricultural associations (Nogyoka1) which emerged existed prima-
rily to compel farmers to conform to the aims of the military. They
served to control agricultural prices and production, to handle collections
and rationing, and to bring in rural capital for the war effort. They
were one of the important means used to police farm communities in the
war effort.
Abolition of this totalitarian structure was recpgnized by GHQ,
SCAP, as a preliminary requisite to fostering and encouraging an agri-
cultural cooperative movement free from domination by nonagrarian in-
terests and dedicated to the economic and cultural advancement of the
Japanese farmer. Law 133, promulgated on 19 November 1947, required the
dissolution. of all of approximately 10,800 incorporated agricultural
associations, and l69,81e incorporated agricultural and sericultural
practice associations by 14 August 1948. After this date these associ-
ations were prohibited from transacting any business except liquidation
of assets. By 31 July 1949, Tiquidation of agricultural associations
was 44 percent complete, while that of the agricultural practice and
sericultural practice associations was 97 percent complete.

The new cooperative. associations organised pursuant to Law 15z which
became effective on 15 December 1947, have as their primary objective
maximum service to participating members rather than payment of dividonde
on invested capital. They are incorporated enterprises, authorised to
perform practically any ecooomic service which will contribute to the
advancement of fard communities. Fifteen or more farmers may organise
a cooperative. Membarship is voluntary. Bach farmer member has one vote
Persons residing within the business area of the organisation may be es-
sociate members with all privileges exoept voting. A farmer is defiped
as any individual who personally carries on or. engages in agriculture.
This definition includes all of those within a'household whose activities
make them eligible, and the participation of women is encouraged. The
business area is as defined by the articles of incorporation. Two or
more cooperatives may organize a federation of agricultural cooperatives.
Such federations may perform any activity which the local oooperatives
are authorized to perform except that if a federation is engaged in the
making of loans and receiving of deposits, it is prohibited from per-
forming additional functions not connected with these activities.
Federations engaging in credit activities may, however, act in behalf of
affiliated associations in discounting bills and guaranteeing debts
towa'rds banking organizations with which such associations are authorised
to.do business.
A cooperative association may charge expensea and reguire contri-:
butions in accordance with its articles of incorporation. It may orgapise
as a capital stock or nonstock association, but no nopstock association
may carry on. both the functions of making loans and receiving deposits.
Liability for a share is limited to the amount of the share and, in the
case of a nonstock organization, the amount of the authorized fee. In
a capital stock association, each member must have at least one share,
and the maximum number of shares which one member may own is specified
in the articles of incorporation. Only members may hold stock. Trans-
fer of stock is. subject to the.approval of the association. Primary
responsibility for carrying out the Agricultural Cooperative Program
is vested in the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry.
As of 30 June 1949, a total of 32,862 new cooperative associations

larger'area, of which 357 were stock and 25 nonstock; 61l federations

16,202 were stock and 15,600 were nonstock.

CROP AND LIVESTOCK ENSURANCE PROGRAK IN JAPAN
Japan's deficit food position requires that agricultural produets
be maintained on land which would be considered submarginal in a surplus
food produotion country. Maintenance of a high level of production,
inclyding use of submarginal land involving high operation risks, is
therefore a matter of national policy and national pecessity. The risks
of operating poor land or land unusually subject to floed or other di
aster are intensified in Japan by the minute scale of Japanese agriculture
and the difficulties faced by a farmer in finding essential substitute
income in the event of even a small-scale crop failure. A farmer must
be guaranteed a minimum return for his efforts or he will cease to farm.
In order to stabilize the economic position of the individual farmer
and to maintain production on a maximum area, the Japanese Goveroment
requires farmers to participate in a compulsory insurance program, and
in return undertakes on behalf of the whole community of consumers as
well as producers to subsidize a part of the cost of insurance benefit
payments.
Japan's present insurance system represents a revision of the
system existing at the time of surrender to bring tt into closer harmopy
with basic Occupation objectives and to correct inherent adminisppetive
and procedural difficulties. Public relief in the event of crop failure
had been recognized in principle since the Tokugawa Era. Rerief in the
form of insurance was instituted in l929 with the passage of the Livestock
Insurance Act and in 1939 with the passage of Crop Insurance Act, Under
these acts responsibility for administration of livestock insurance
rested with voluntary associations organized for the purpose, but crop
insurance, which was compulsory, was carried on by agricultural associ-
ations. These have been dissolved as part of the agrarian refoim program
and replaced with democratically organized cooperative associations.
Development of a new administrative mechanism to replace that provided
by the dissolved organizations thus became necessary. Moreover, preex.
isting insurance procedures were defective in a number of ways. Pro-
cedures for fixing crop insurance premiums failed to differentiate
between high- and low-risk areas. Farmers in low-risk areas thus had
to assume a part of cost of insuring crops in the higher-risk regions.
Premium rates were insufficient to finance insurance obligations, and a
substantial deficit had accumulated. Both livestock and crop insurance
systems were established to pay losses in amounts which, under present
economic conditions, are insufficient to be effective in maintaining
maximum agricultural production.
The new Compensation Against Agricultural Loss Law, promulgated
15 December l947, substitutee a unified compensation system for the dual
system previously in effect. Its object is to maintain maximum agricul-
tural production. It calls for determination of premium payments on the
1

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.98)
as possible from premiums paid by mombera.: Lossds in'exoess of an
collected as premiums are met by government subsidy. The prineiple
bubsidy is establishéd partly in recogmition of the mation's reepomeibiltt
to participate in costs incurred for benefits which acorue to the matiom
And partly to keep premiums within the farmers' capaoity to pay. Imsurame
of silkworms, oocoons, paddy and upland rice,,wheat, barley, and ats le
compulsory. Livestock insurance is voluntary, unless an individual assnel
ation makes it compulsory by majority votes at a general. meeting. Fam
buildings, implements and miscellaneous crops may be insured on a voluntary
basis. ur
Compulsory crop insurance covers. one-half of the estimated average
production per tan of crops. . Additional coverage is theoretically per-
missive but has not been started. Maximum payments cover one-half of the
losses in excess of 30 percent (40 percent for sericulture.) No payments
are made for losses of less than 30 percent (40 percent for sericulture).
Livestock insurance coverage on adult animals is limited to 80
percent of the value of the animals. On embryo and newborn animals,
maximum coverage is 16 percent of the value of the parent. he rate in-
creases up to 30 percent with the age of the animal.
Administration of the law at the local level rests with agricultural
mutual relief associations organized under the supervision of prefeotural
governors., At the prefectural level, responsibility for administration
rests with federations of agricultural mutual relier associations organized
under the supervision of the Minister of Agriculture and Forestry. Local
associations insure with prefectural associations, which in turn reinaure
with the national government. General surveillance over the entire struoture
is maintained by the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry to insure com-
pliance with the law.

out of an eventual total of 10,88l. By that date, each prefecture had

insurance,:531 county associations, and an unreported number of local

insurance covered 306,658 head of cattle, horses, goats, sheep, and swine
as of 5 March 1949, insured for ¥3,003,134,920. Premiums paid totalled
¥62,661,359 against which ¥22,718,432 in benefits had been paid.

AGRICULTURE IN JAPAN
Introduction
1. The two most important agricultural problems confronting the
Occupation in Japan have been insufficient food production and the low
economic status of the rural community. Topography and the pressure of
a large farm population on a limited acreage have had much to do with
the relatively small area under cultivation, and with the amall, un-
economic farm units, widespread tenancy, and the low standard of living
of those who till the soil. The Japanese are good farmers, but even thoir
skill cannot overcome the limitations imposed by a total of only 15 million
acres of cultivated land and 82 million people. In the past Japan could
not attain food self-sufficiency even in years of abundant crops, but has
had to rely on imports to make good the deficit. Now that former overseas
sources of supply are no longer available, maximizing food produotion is
one of Japen's most urgent needs.
2. The scarcity of arable land in Japan gave rise to many abuses in
the land tonure system and in the field of agricultural taxe and credit.
These abuses, coupled with domination of agriculture by bureaucratic and
nonagrarian interests, multiplied the difficulties of the Jepanese farer
in attaining a level of economic security comparable to the remainder of
the population. National policies designed to expand industrial production
and foroign trade rolegatod agriculture to an inferior position in the
oconomy. Those dependont upon the land for their livelihood did not sharo
to any substantial degree in the improved standard of living that resulted
from the increased national income.
3. The low economic status of the Japeneso farmers over the yoars
has creeted among that group & foeling of hopelessness and restlossness.
Agrarian unrest in turn hes provided no smell part of the stimulus to
Japan's imperialistic foreign policy. Tonants particularly, end fafmers
generally, have been in lerge measure sympathetic to oxtremist political
movements by Japan's militaristic exponents.
4. In recognition of tho nature of Japan's economy, the Occupation
has directed its agricultural programs toward two major objectives,
namely, maximum food production, and the echievemont of social and
oconomic stability of tho farm population. The briof report that follows
outlinos tho nature and scope of the agriculturel progrems institutod.

SUMMARY
Production Statistica
Stimulated by expansion of trade and population, Japan increased it
staple food output 96 percent between the 1880's and the decade immediately
preceding World War II. Rice and wheat accounted for most of this gain.
At the end of World War II hostilities, however, many of its people faced
the threat of starvation. By 1945 fertilizer shortages, combined with bad
weather and other disrupting influences, had reduced harvests. Since
that time production has gradually increased. In 1948 staple food crop
harvests were six percent above the 193l-40 average, but not as high as
e lo3c record cron. Incrense came chiefly from sweet and white potatoes
and barley.
In the period extending from the 1880's to 1921, Japan had expanded
its cultivuted areas from 12 percent of the total land mass to 16 percent,
resulting in a total cultivated area of about 15,000,000 acres. After
that time the area remained relatively constant until the war, when
military requisitions reduced it by about 500,000 acres.
Annual harvest of minor cereals, beans, and peas followed the upward
trend of the staple crops until about l920, but declined thereafter. The
trend in vegetable und fruit prcduction continued upward for a longer
period. However, with major emphasis on staple food crops during and since
the war, the trend reversed. In 1f4? production ranged from 23 to 40
percent less thun prewar for these groups.
Among industrial crops, mulberry for silkworms (which more than
doubled in average annual acreape from the 1881-90 decade to the 1931-40
decade) has been cut back during and since the war to less than two-thirds
of the lael-90 figure. This decrease has been due to loss of foreign silk
markets und need for lund for food producci on. Ten production is little
more than one-half of the prewar uverage. On the cther hand, the 1948
tobucco crop was 42 percent more than in 1931-40.
Fertilizer
With soils naturally low in fertility and with intensive cropping,
Janan must rely heavily on fertilizers. When World War II begnn, the
nation had ample fucilities to produce most of its nitrogenous and phosphatic
commercial fertilizers but had to import phosphatic rock. Limited equipment
and lick of indigenous deposits mnde it necessary also to ship in the
inorganic potash used. Peak fertilizer producticn wus reached during the

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.99)
period 1936-40. When war hostilities ended, the fertiliser indumt
4 serious condition. Since then commercial. fertiliser supplies
ateadily. Largely as a result of action by Allied authorities, ine
Imports of large quantitie's and introduction of measures to inorease d
production, the supply of nitrogenous fertilizer for farm consumption
risen from 439,000 metric tons in fiscal year 1946 to 1,464,000 metrio to
in l949; phosphatic fertilizer used by farmers increased during that porie
from 32,156 metric tons to 1,033,125 metric tons, and potash supplies during
the same period have mounted from 902 metric tons to 159,000 metrio tons.
A system of fertilizer allocations begun during the war has been
recast by the Occupation to insure timely deliveries and equftable distri-
bution between crops and areas. Importance of compost, animal manures, and
pight soil in crop production has been emphasized continuously. Current use
of compost and night soil is estimated to be above the l936-40 average.
Insect and Disease Control
Plant insects and diseases, although estimated to reduce crops in Japan
by at least 12 percent annually, received minor control.attention :prior to
1947. While some good resulted from laboratory investigations, field work
had been neglected. Use of pesticides in the dust form was practically
unknown. Insecticide supplies, reduced during the war, have been adequate
in 1949. The Agriculture Division of Natural Resources Section has encouraged
experimental work on insect control, the use of dusts, and the use of new
insecticides. DDT and benzene hexachloride, newly developed insecticides whiph
can be produced in Japan, have relieved the pressure on other chemicals, par-
ticularly diesel oil and pyrethrum,for control of rice leaf hoppers. Other
insecticides have been imported for testing. In addition to larger supplies
and more effective kinds of pesticides, efforts are being made to improve.
control practices. The principle and use of insecticide dusts and aerosols
are being emphasized by the Agriculture Division to obtain more effective
control of pests. An Agricultural Chemical Control Law was promulgated in
1948 to assist in overcoming the inferior quality of pesticides. A Plant.
Quarantine and Emergency Pest Control measure will be presented to the next
session of the Diet. The Agriculture Division of Natural Resources Section
is assisting the Japanese Government in the programs discussed above and is
sponsoring an extensive research program designed to develop more effective
controls of plant pests, particularly rice borers.
Seed Situation
Improved seed is a major factor affecting crop production in Japan.
Methods of plant breeding, and seed increase, inspection, certification, and
distribution as means of increasing food production have been greatly improved
during the past few years. However, hybridization and back-orossing have only
recently received attention from Japanese scientists. Long-range inoreases
in yields through use of these techniques may be expected.
With advice and guidance from the Agriculture Division, Natural Resources
Section, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry has made pronounced advances
in the production of certified seed of white potatoes since the Occupation
began. Seven foundation seed farms were established in 1947 and increased

Livestock
Livestock plays a mihor role as a diract food in Japan. Before loda
gation consumed almost no meat or milk. Up to l94l animal foods, othor
fish, amounted to only a little more than one percent of the total foog be
sumed. Even this amount decreased during and after World War II, largely
because of lack of concentrate feeds. Horses and draft cattle are necosemry
to provide farm draft power, the present number of draft animals is condidered
aufficient for that purpose. More dairy cows, dairy goats, and sheep are.
needed for inoreased production of protein food and other produots, swine
have increased markedly in recent years despite the shortage of concentrate
fleeds. Poultry has regained only a slight portion of the 70 percent doorease
it suffered during the period 1941-46.
Under SCAP direction, the livestock control companies were dissolved.
Government livestock breeding stations are still maintained, although thesr
need has not been demonstrated. The Agriculture Division of Natural.Resourees
Section is encouraging policies to establish an'economically sdund livestook
program. These policies include increased indigenous feed productions o
pecially improved forage production) to the extent possible without deorarming
the amount of staple food grown, and livestock breeding production and utili-
zation by private enterprise, at maximum levels consistent with feed imports.
Government aid in the form of improved research and education facilitfes and
farmer access to long-and short-term credit at low interest rates are alme
included in these policies:
Food Storage and Preservation
The Japanese climate, with hot humid summers and rains in the lete sprind
and fall harvest seasons, magnifies food storage and preservation problomse
The six million tons of sweet potatoes deluging the fall markets present an
especial difficulty. Storage facilities are inadequate, and freesing weather
in winter prevents later shipment. Grain storage is adequate in capaoity but
is not.rodent-proof or air-conditioned. Vegetables are preaerved by salting.
pickling, or drying, but major lossee result: during market movements from
lack of refrigeration and shipping facilities, At the instigition of the
Agriculture Division, the Japanese are paying partioular atteption 'to potato
spoilage problems. Sweet potato storage on farms has received increasing
emphasis since 1947; the government will pay 70 percent more for farm-stored
sweet potatoes after 1 March 1950 than during the peak season. As a result of
Agriculture Division recommendation's, a five-year plan has been launched to

Recognising land reclamation and improvement as a basio meane

Soil Survey
Basic knowledge of the soils is essential to proper evaluating of
Japanese agriculture, and to establishment of policy and development of pro-
grams for increasing food and feed crops. The first soil maps of Japan were
made under a program instituted in 1882, and by 1945 all cultivated land,
except parts of Hokkaido, had been mapped. In 1936, however, aware of the
inadequacies of their soil maps, Japanese soil scientists began the study of
foreign classification methods. Few-maps were completed under the revised
scheme. A reconnaissance soil survey was initiated by the Natural Resources
Section late in 1945. The survey, by United States soil scientists, makes
use of an internationally known system of classifying soils by the morphological
characteristics of the soil profile. Field work has been completed, and
preparation of a series of eight reports is now in progress.

system of food collection from farmers and rationing to nonfarm families.

Agrioultural Research
Japan's lack of sufficient arable land to provide food for
population makes an effective agricultural research system im
Insure highest returns from thè agrioultural resources. When 
began, research work was soattered, with little coordination, mmons
Kundred experiment stations and research institutions. The erran
gesulted in duplication of effort and inefficient use of funda,
equipment was inadequate in many of the stations.
With advice and guidance of specialists in the Agriculture Divimion
Natural Resources Section, the Japanese Diet enacted legislation in los8 to
provide for elimination of nonessential stations, consolidation of researoh
institutions, application of research to immediate and practical farm
problems, and prompt dissemination of findings to farmers. An Agrioultural

design methods only recently known to the Japanese.
Agricultural Extension
Planned distribution of technical knowledge to farmers in Japan was
provided by legislation enacted in l899, establishing a government-controlled
aystem of agricultural associations to improve crop practices. Under this
system, the government provided for one or more technicians in each village
to work with farmers. In addition, cooperatives, organized under a law of
1900, helped finance operations of farms to supply superior seed and, in
some instances, employed technicians to advise farmer members.
During World War II the associations and cooperatives were combined
into a national association which was given control over crop quotas, ool-
lections, and retioning of critical. materials. Agricultural technicians
were given control and policing duties., The national control organization
and its member associations were dissolved by direction of the Oooupation
authorities.
Legislation enacted in July 1948 on advice of Agriculture Division
specialists provided for an agricultural advisory service. Prefectural
governments are completing the organization of Agricultural Improvement
Sections to work with the national government in developing such a system.
More than 6,O00 village farm and home advisors have been appointed, and
more than 3,300 rural youth clubs have been organized. AgricuIture Division
specialists are working with the Japanese on plans for on-the-job-training
programs to develop advisory techniques and leadership skills.

Agricultural Cooperatives
The minute size of the average Japanese farm management, unit requiree
that: farmers pool their efforts and resources through cooperation. The prmr
Japanese Government took advantage of this situation to codatruct e monopollstio.
hierarchy of agricultural associations through which the government contrelled
almost all aspects of farm life. Membership was compulsory for ell .farmers.
SCAP policy has required dissolution of this basically militaristio
set-up and its replacement. by a genuine agricultural cooperative.movement
free from domination by nonagrarian interests and dedicated to the ecenomle
and cultural advancement of the farmer.  The Agricultural Cooperative Associ-
ation Law which became effective on 15 December l947, providas for the.ergan-
ization of democratic agricultural cooperatives based on internationally reoog-
nized cooperative principles. By 30 June 1949 a total of 31 329 looal and
individual cooperatives had been organized. These had federated intere total
of 993 federations of agricultural cooperatives by the same' dataa  Volume of.
busihess' records for the year 1948 showed that cooperatives handled a major
portion of farmer purchases, sales, financial depbsits, and Ioans. Membermhip
in: the'se cooperatives 1s voluntary. Each member has one vote. The primary
purpose of the cooperatives is maximum service to participating members rather
than payment of dividends on invested capital. Results of officer elections
7

=== Page 315 === (confidence 0.94)
for these organizations in March through May 1949 show that over 80 percent of
the present officers have never been directors or auditors in the former agri-
cultural associations. This indicates a substantial change of leadership in
rural communities.
Problems remaining include improving the cperating cfficiency and placing
the new organizations on a sound financiul basis, building memberahip loyalty,
retaining the present freedom from pclitical control, and strengthening demo-
cratic management.

MISSION AND ACCOMPLISHBOENTS OF THE GOCUPATION IN THE NATURAL MESOURCES
FIELD
I. INTRODUCTION
1. Production from all the natural resourcee of Japan is the pri-
mary concern of Matural Resources Section. Its field of operations in-
oludes most of the land ares of Japan, and the surrounding seas as well.
The significance in the life of the Japanese of the materials and aotiv-
ities with which the Matural Resources Section is concerned can hardly
be overestimated.
2. 0f the 91.000.000 aores making up Japan. 17.000,000 aares (19
percent) is agrioultural land. About 15.000.000 mcres of this land is
under cultivation, and the remainder is pasture and range land. Forest
areas of 62,000,000 acres account for 68 percent of the total, and lakes
and rivers l.5 percent. The remainder coneiste of inhabited areas. roads.
and wasteland such as mountains above the timberline. These factors re-
veal that a study of the fields covered by the Matural Resourcee Section
involves a study of nearly 90 percent of the total land surface of Japan.
3. The present population of Japan (September 1949) 1s estimated
to be 80.000,000 persons. The Japanese labor force over the age of 14
years totals roughly 36,000,000 persons. About 18.000,000 of shese
workers are engaged in agriculture and forestry. Another 6l0.000 are
engaged full time in fishing and related pursuits, and 500.000 in mining
and the smelting and refining of metals. Thus 53 peroent of the total
adult working population of Japan is engaged in farming, fishing. for-
estry, or mining. If part-time employees. seasonal employees, and
children were considered, these figares would be greatly magnified. In
the United States these fields include only 35 percent of the total
population.
4. Matural Resources Section deals, in large measure, with the
primary products of the country - the items produced from approximasely
90 percent of the land area. and from the seas. These products consist
of food from agrioulture, fisheries, and wildlife, construction mate-
rials, fuel for heating. cooking, and transportation, and mammfacturing
materiale from foreste and mines. From these resources must come the
largest possible supply for the Japanese population, and material for
export as well. In addition to its interest in the basic resources of
the country. Matural Resources Section is concerned with the democra-
tisation and economic welfare of the people whose livelihood comes from
farming. fishing, forestry, and mining - more than one-half of the popu-
lation.
5. In all of the production fields outlined above, thie Section's
mission is (1) to maximise immediate production and promote efficient
utilisation of the materiale produced in order to minimise import re-
quirements, and (2) to maximise long-range production in order to reduce
Japan's future dependence on foreign sources for its supplies.
6. Matural Resources Section was organised with four technical
divisions, to cover agricultural, fishery, forestry, and mining and
geologioal activities, respectively, and the necessary service divisions.
After four yeare of experience, the organisation is as shown in 
Chart 1.
7. Japanese food production is very important not only to Japan
bus also to the United States, for we have taken the responsibility of
