9. With responsibilities covering nerrly 90 percent of the land
area, 53 peroent of the adult working population, and 95 peroent of the
funds used for relief. Batural Resources Section has realised the urgent
need for detailed sudy of all probleme bearing upon the resouroes situ-
ation. The following general desoription of each of the four fields
covered  this Section sets forth the objectives pursued. progress made.
and the programs under way at present.
II. AGRICULTURE
1. Introduction: a. The two most important agricultural problems
confronting the Occupation in Japan have been ineufficient food production
and the low economic status of the rural commmnity. Topography and the
pressure of a large farm population on a limited acreage have had much to
do with the relatively small aree under cultivatien, and with the small,
meconomic farm units, widespread tenancy, and the low standard of living
of those whe till the soil. The Japanese are good farmers, but even
their skill cannot overcome the limitations imposed by a total of oaly
15 million acres of cultivated land and 82 million people. In the past
Japan could not attain food self-sufficiency even in years of abundant
crops, but has had to rely on imports to make good the deficit. Now
that former overseas sources of supply are no longer available, maxi-
mising food production is one of Japan's most urgent needs.
b. The scarcity of arable land in Japan gave rise to many
abuses in the land tenure system and in the field of agricultural taxss
and credit. These abuses, coupled with domination of agriculture by
3

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.98)
bureancratic and nonagrarian interests, maltiplied the difficalties of
the Japanese farmer in attaining a level of economic security comparable
to the remainder of the population. National policies designed te expand
industrial production and foreign trade relegated agriculture te am infe-
rior position in the economy. Those dependent upon the land for their
livelihood did not share to any substantial degree in the improved
standard of living that resulted from the inoreased national imoeme.
o. The low economic status of the Japanese farmere over the
years has crented among that group a feeling of hopelessmess and rest-
lessness. Agrarian unrest in turn has provided no mall part of the
stimulue to Japan's imperialistic foreign policy. Tenante partioalarly.
and farmers generally, have been in large measure sympathetic to estres-
ist political movements by Japan's militaristic exponents.
d. In recognition of the nature of Japan's economy. the Osou-
pation has directed its agricultural programs toward two major objeo-
tives, namely, maximum food production, and the achievement of secial and
economic stability of the farm population. The brief report that follows
outlines the nature and scope of the agricultural programe instituted.
2. Production Statistice: a. Stimalated ty expansion of trade
and population. Japan increased its staple food output 96 percent between
the l880'e and the decade immediately preceding World War II. Mice and
wheat accounted for most of this gain. At the end of World War II hos-
tilities, however, many of its people faced the threat of starvasion.
By 1945 fertiliser shortages, combined with bad weather and other dis-
rupting influences, had reduced harvests. Since that time prodnction
has sradually increased. In l948 staple food crop harvests were six
peroent above the 1931-40 average. but not as high as the 1930 recerd
crop. Increase came chiefly from sweet and white potatoes and barley.
b. In the period extending from the 1880's to 1921, Japan had
expanded its cultivated areas from 12 percent of the total land mase to
16 percent, resulting in a total cultivated ares of about 15,000.000
mores. After that time the area remained relatively constant until the
war, when military requisitions reduced it by about 500.000 acres.
c. Annual harvests of minor cereals, beans, and peas folloved
the upward trend of the staple crops until about l920. but declined
thereafter. The trend in vegetable and fruit production continued up-
ward for a longer period. However, with major emphasis on staple food
crops during and since the war, the trend reversed. In 194s production
ranged from 23 to 40 percent less than prewar for these sroups.
d. Among industrial crops, mulberry for silkworms (which more
than doubled in average annual acrenge from the 188l-90 decade to the
1931-40 decade) has been out back during and since the war to less than
two-thirds of the l88l-90 figure. Thie decrease hae been due to lose of
foreign silk markets and need for land for food production. Tea prodno-
tion is little more than one-half of the prewar average. On the other
hand, the 1948 tobacco crop was 42 peroent more than in 1931-40.
3. Fertiliser: a. With soils naturally low in fertility and
with intensive oropping, Japan mst rely heavily on fertilisers. When
World War II began, the nation had ample facilities to produce most of
its nitrogenous and phosphatic commeroial fertilisere but had to import
phosphatic rock. Limited equipment and lack of indigenous deposits made
it necessary also to ship in the inorganic potash used. Peak fertiliser
production was reached during the period 1936-40. When war hostilities
4

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.97)
ended, the fertiliser industry was in a serious condition. Since then
commercial fertiliser supplies have increased steadily. largely as a
result of action by Allied authorities. including importe of large quan-
tities and introduction of measures to increase domestie productios.
The supply of nitrogemous fertiliser for fars consumption has risem from
439,000 metric tons in fiscal year 1946 to 1.464,000 metric toms im 1949;
phosphatic fertiliser used by farmere increased during that peried frem
32,156 metric tons to l.033,125 metric tons, and potash supplies consased
have mounted from 903 metrie tons to 159,000 metric tons.
b. A yetem of fertiliser alloontione, begun during the war.
has been recast by the Ocoupation to insure timely deliveries and equi-
table distribution between crops and areas. Importance of compost, ani-
mal mamares, and night soil in crop production has been emphasised con-
timmously. Ourrent use of compost and night soil is estimated to be
above the 1936-40 average.
4. Inssct and Disease Control: Plant insecte and diseases. al-
though estimated to reduce crops in Japan by at lenst 12 peroens annually.
received minor control astention prior to l947. While some good resalted
from laboratory investigations, field work had been neglected. Use of
pesticides in the dust form was practically unknown. Insecticide sup-
plies, reduced during the war, have been adequate in l949. Eperimental
work has been encouraged on insect control, the use of dusts, and the use
of new insecticides. DDt and bensene hexachloride. newly developed
insecticides which can be produced in Japan, have relieved the pressure
on other chemicale, particularly diesel oil and pyrethrum for control of
rice leaf heppers. Other insecticides have been imported for testing.
In addition to larger sapplies and more effective kinds of pesticide.
efforts are being made to improve control practices. The principle and
use of inseoticide duste and aerosols are being emphasised to obtain more
effective control of pests. An Agricultural Chemical Control Law was
promalgated in l94s to assist in overcoming the inferior quality of pes-
tioides. A Plant Quarantine and mergenay Pest Control measure will be
presented to the next session of the Diet. R is assisting the Jspamese
Government in the programs discuesed above and is sponsoring an extensive
research program designed to develop more effective controls of plant
pests, particularly rice borers.
5. Seed Situations a. Improved seed is a major factor affecting
crop production in Japan. Methods of plant breeding. and seed inorease
inspection. certification, and distribution as means of increasing food
prodnction have been greatly improved under the guidance of Matural Re-
sources Section. Eybridisation and back-crossing have only recently
received attention from Japanese scientists, and long-range increase in
yields through these techniques may be expected.
b. Under the Occupation, pronounced advances have been made
in the production of certified seed of white potatoes. Seven foundation
seed farme were established in l947 and incressed emphasis placed on
seed production. This program is reflected in banner potato production
in 1948 and 1949 despite inclement weather over much of the potato area
of Japan during the 1949 growing season.
c. Botable improvement has also been made in the selection.
curing. and storing of seed of sweet potatoes. Nethods of curing intro-
dnced ty Matural Resources Section now permit long-time storage and the
planting of sound seed, and are paving the way for improving the main
orop storage.
5

=== Page 324 === (confidence 0.97)
6. Lávestock; a. Livestock playe a minor role as a direet food
in Japan. Before l8ss, the nation consumed almost no meat or milk. Up
to 194l animal foods, other than fish, amounted to only a litsle more
than one peroent of the total food consumed. Even this amount deereased
during and after World War II. largely becanse of lack of concemtrate
feeds. Horses and draft cattle are necessary to provide farm drafts pewer:
the present mumber of draft animals is considered sufficient for that par
pose. Nore dairy cows, dairy goats, and sheep are needed for inoreesed
production of protein food and other products. Swine have imereased
markedly in recent yeare despite the shortage of concentrate feeds.
Poultry has regained only a slight portion of the 70 peroent desrease it
suffered during the period 1941-46.
b. Under Sdap direction, the livestock control compamies were
diesolved. Government livestook breeding stations are still mmintaimed.
although their need has not been demonstrated.  is encouraging policies
to establish an economically sound livestock progras. These policies in-
clude inoreased indigenous feed production (especially improved forage
production), to the extent possible without decreasing the amouss of staple
food grown and livestook breeding prodnction and utilisation  private
enterpriees, at maximm levels consistent with feed imports. Govermmest
aid in the form of improved research and education facilities, and farmer
accese to long and short term credit at low interest rates are aleo in-
cluded in these policies.
7. Pood Storege and Preservation: The Japanese climate, with hot
hamid summers and rains in the late spring and fall harvest seasons.
magnifies food storage and preservation problems. The six million tome
of sweet potatoes deluging the fall markets present an especial diffi-
culty. Storage facilities are inndequate, and freesing weather in
winter prevents later shipment. Grain storage is adequate in ompacity
but is not rodent-proof or air-conditioned. Vegetables are preserved
by salting, pickling, or drying, but major losses remult during market
movements from lack of refrigeration and shipping facilities. The Jap-
anese are now paying particular attention to potato poilage problems.
Sweet potato storage on farms has been receiving increasing emphasis
since l947; the govemment will pay 70 percent more for farm-stored
sweet potatoes after 1 March 1950 than during the peak season. A five-
year plan has been launched to provide llc,000 metrio tons additional
space for potatoes each year. Currently 215 buildinge to provide an
annual storage capacity of about 25,000 metrio tons are under con-
struotion.
8. Land Reclamation and Improvement: a. Recognizing land reole-
mation and improvement as a besic means of incressing food production.
the Japanese Government has provided subsidies for this activity for
many decades. Nevertheless, land reclaimed from l910 to l939 averaged
slightly less than 30,000 mores per year; gains from reclamation were
largely offset by divereions of agrioultural land to other uses. From
1937 to 1944, because of military acquisition, cultivated areas de-
creased about 500,000 acres. After the surrender, the government placed
1ncreased emphasis on land reclamation. Under SCaP direction the Land
Reform Law was written; it inaluded authority for the sovernment to
acquire uncultivated land for reclamation and established procedures for
sale of reclaimed land to cultivators. A new Land Improvement Lew.
promulgated in June l949, provides for more effective land improvement
conducted  associations of the looal people concerned.
b. With the inoreased emphasis on reclamation since 1945, the
covernment has mcquired 2.7 million mcres of unoultivated land for re-
olamation and 845,000 mores have been reclaimed.
6

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.97)
c. sOap consultante have, assisted the Jmpanese authorisies mot
only in implementing legislation and instructions but also in the solu-
tion of technical problems in mctual field work. Guidance has bees
siven in developing standards for the selection of land so be reelaimed.
in planming and settling reclaimed areas, and in designing and construo-
ting land improvement projects.
9. Soil Survey: Bmsic knowledge of the soile is essential to
proper evaluation of Japanese agriculture, and to establishment of pol-
ioy and development of programs for increasing food and feed crops. The
first soil maps of Japan were made under a program instituted is lses, and
by l945 all cultivated land, except parts of Mokkaido, had beem mepped.
In 1936, however, aware of the inadequscies of their soil maps, Japanese
soil scientists began the study of foreign olassifioation methods. Few
mape were completed under the revised scheme. A reconnaissance seil
survey was initiated under the Occupation in late 1945. The earvey. W
United States soil scientists, makes use of an internationally kmows
system of classifying soils by the morphological characterissies of the
soil profile. Field work has been ompleted, and preparation of a series
of sight reports is now in progress.
10. Pood Collection: a. As the Japanese Sovernment tighsemed its
economic and other controls in the years immediately preceding and during
World War II. it instituted a aystem of food collection from famere and
rationing to non-farm families. Only rice was collected in the first
year - l940. Wheat and barleys were added in 194l and sweet potatoes and
white potatoes in l942. The system was used through she remaining war
years. Food shortages necessitated continuance ty the Occupasiom of the
collection and rationing aystes. Use of the syetem apparently will be
necessary for a number of years to come.
b. About 80 percent of all staple crops is subject to colleo-
tion. Bafore planting time each year, the Ministry of Agrioultare and
Forestry, on the basis of "normal" production, determines delivery quotas
and assigas them to prefectures. The prefectural sovernmente sub-allot
the quotas to counties (guns) and cities; the counties sub-allot them so
towns and villages, which in turn assign them to individual farmers.
Quotas may be revised downward at harvest time if crop conditions warrant.
Authority to increase quotas at harvest time will be considered at the
next session of the Mational Diet.
c. Coneumer goode in short supply are made available on a
priority basis to farmers as an incentive to stimulate crop deliveries.
d. The 1942-44 war years showed a continuously high total
collection level, followed by a sharp drop in 1945 and progreasive in-
creases through 194s. Deliveries of each of the staple crops in 1948
exceeded those of l947. The outlook for 1949 collections is encouraging.
. The Matural Resources Section maintains close and continuing
surveillance over all phases of the food collection program, including
food production, assignment and revision of collection quotas, and actual
collectione by the Japanese Government.
11. Crop Reporting: a. In l878 Japan began to collect national
statistics on area and production of all major crops, based on reports
from prefectural sovermment officials. Field sampling to determine yield
at harvest time was commonly used by local crop surveyors as early as
1933, but prior to l947 little attempt was made to forecast production
ahead of harvest.
7

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.98)
b. A lucrative "black merket". and fear of motual henger grew-
ing out of the war served as incentives for farmere and local affieials
to underreport both crop plantings and yielde. As a result, the mcoaracy
of national crop ares and production reports was impaired. Following a
study by an expert from the United States in 1946. a new orop repersing
system was set up. with control and supervision centralised in the Mimis-
try of Agriculture and Forestry. Under this systes, the acouragy ef
farmers' reports on acreage of crops is measured by means of sample sar-
veye, and yielde are determined by field sampling. These operatioms are
conducted by trained personns) in prefectural offices of the Ministry of
Agrioulture and Forestry. Acreage surveys in l948 accounted for more than
a million acres of staple crope that farmers had failed to report.
c. Japan's food deficit must be met by U.s. appropriation. so
a reliable crop reporting system is mandatory. The present ystem is s-
perior to that used prior to l948, but many problems remain. mffective
guidance eventually should place the Japanese crop reporting system on a
high plane of efficiency and effectiveness.
12. Aricultural Ressarch: a. Japan' lack of sufficient arable
land to provide food for her growing population takes an effective agri-
cultural research system imperative to ineure highest returne from the
agricultural resources. When the Oocupation began, research work was
scattered, with little coordination, among eeveral hundred experiment
stations and research institutions. The arrangement resulted in dupli-
cation of effort and inefficient use of funds. Further. equipment was
inadequate in many of the stations.
b. On advice of Matural Resources Section specialists. the
Japanese Diet enacted legielation in l948 to provide for elimination of
nonessential stations, consolidation of research institutions, sppli-
cation of research to imediate and practical farm problems. and promps
dissemination of findings to farmers. An Agricultural Improvement Bu-
rean was established in the Winistry of Agriculture and Forestry in Augast
1948. A plan for consolidating research institutione was approved by the
Cabinet in July 1949 and 1s soheduled for accomplishment by 1 April 1950;
under this plan 135 stations will be closed and 53 will be combined with
the l9l remmining. Matural Resources Section specialists conducted a
school in experimental methods in March 1949. Under the guidanoe of these
pscialists various experiments have been conducted which utilise experi-
mental design methode only recently known to the Japanese.
13. Agricultural Extension: a. Planned distribution of technical
knowledge to farmere in Japan was provided by legislation enacted in l899
establishing a government-controlled system of agricultural associations
to improve crop practices. Under this system the government provided for
one or more technicians in each village to work with farmers. In addition.
cooperatives, organised under a law of l900, helped finance operations of
farms to supply superior seed and in some instances employed teohnicians
to advise farmer members.
b. During World War II the associations and coopemtives were
combined into a national association which was siven control over crop
quotas, collections, and rationing of critical materials. Agricultural
technicians were given control and policing duties. The national control
organisation and its member associatione were dissolved by direction of
the Ocoupation authorities.
c. Legielation enacted in July l948 provided for an agrioul-
tural advisory service. Prefectural sovernments are completing the
organisation of Agricultural Improvement Sections to work with the
8

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.98)
national government in developing such a syetem. More than 6,000 village
farm and home advisors have been appointed, and more than 3,300 rural
youth clube have been organised. Matural Resources Section specialiste
are working with the Japanese on plans for on-the-job-training programs
to develop advisory techniques and leadership skills.
14. Land Reform: a. Prior to the Occupation 70 percent of the
Japanese farmers rented all or part of the land they cultivated. The
pressure of population on limited land resources perpetuated a feudal-
istic land temure system requiring payment of exorbitant rents in kind
often amounting to 50 to 70 percent of grose farm production. Japanese
land reform legislation enacted by the Diet and promulgated 21 October
1946 has two main objectivee: (1) tranefsr f  ownership to farmers
who actually till the soil and (2) improvement o: farm tenancy practices
for those who contimue as terants. The program emphasises private indi-
vidual ownership of land rather than state or collective ownership.
During the initial transfer phase, which is virtually completed. almost
one-third of the cultivated land in Japan - more than 4.5 million mores
involving about 30 million tracts - has been purchased by the govermment
from former land ownere for resale to cultivators. An estimated 12 per-
cent of the cultivated land will remain in tenancy to provide the neces-
sary amount of tenure flexibility. The land reform program aleo includes
provisions to ineure fair tenancy practices for the remmining tenants
such as written farm leases, rent ceilings, cash rentale, restrictions
on land transfer, and safeguarde againet arbitrary changes in rental
contracts. Ourrent operatione include the registration of title trane-
fers, collection and payment for lands sold and purchased by the govern-
ment, and continued improvement of tenancy practices.
b. The Matural Resources Section provided technical advice and
guidance in the preparation of the legislation on which the land reform
program is based. Specialists onsulted with Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry officials, furnishing advice and guidance on administrative
policies adopted and maintaining contimuous contact with the progress of
all aotivities.
15. Agricultural Cooperatives: a. The minute size of the average
Japanese farm management unit requires that farmere pool their efforts and
resources through cooperation. The prewar Japanese government took ad-
vantage of this situation to construct a monopolistic hierarchy of agri-
cultural associations through which the government controlled almost all
aspeots of farm life. Membership was compulsory for all farmers.
b. SCaP policy has required dissolution of this basically
militaristic set-up and ite replacement by a genuine agrioultural cooper-
ative movement free from domination by nonagrarian interests and dedi-
cated to the economic and cultural advancement of the farmer. The Agri-
cultural Cooperative Association Lav, which became effective on 15 De-
cember l947, provided for the organisation of democratic agricultural
cooperatives based on internationally recognised cooperative principles.
By 30 June 1949 a total of 31,329 local and individual cooperatives had
been organised. These had federated into a total of 993 federations of
agricultural cooperatives by the same date. Volume of business records
for the year l948 showed that cooperatives handled a major portion of
farmer purchases, sales, financial deposits, and loans. Nemberehip in
these cooperatives is voluntary. Each member has one vote. The primary
purpose of the cooperatives is maximum service to participating members
rather than payment of dividends on invested capital. Resulte of officer
electione for these organisations in March through May 1949 show that
over 80 percent of the present officers have never been directors or
anditors in the former agricultural associations. This indicates a
subetantial change of leadership in rural communities.
9

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.98)
c. Problems remaining include improving the operating effi-
ciengy and placing the new orgamisations on a sound financial basis.
building membership loyalty, retaining the present freedom from political
control, and strengthening democratic manngement.
16. Crop and Livestock Inurance: a. Japan's deficit food position
requires that agricultural production be maintained on land which would
be considered submarginal in a country which prodnces surpluses of food.
Compulsory crop insurance, supported in part by government subsidy, and
voluntary livestock insurance have been in operation with limited bene-
fits since 1929 and 1939 respectively as measures required to keep sub-
marginal land in production.
b. Japan's present insurance aystem, as authorised in the law
of 15 Decenber 1945 for Compensation Against Agricultural Loss, 1s a re-
vision of the systen existing at the time of surrender. The changes were
designed to bring the aystem into harmony with basic Occupation Objectives
and to correct administrative and procedural difficulties. Insurance cov-
erage on staple food crops is compulsory in order to guarantee mmximm
effectiveness of the progrem in terme of crop production. Premiugs are
fixed for each area in accord with that area's past record of losses.
Livestock insurance is voluntary unless the general membership of the
local agricultural mutual association votes to make it compulsory.
Insurance of other property and prodnce is optional. Administr-tion is
placed in local agricultural mutual relief associations operated by
farmers, and in prefectural federations of these local associations. The
Japanese Government exercises only linited general supervision.
c. Agricultural mutual relief associations establiahed on a
local basis numbered 10,663 as of 30 April 1949. Bach prafecture had
one federation. Local associations insure with prefectural federations,
which in turn reinsure with the national government. Losses in excess
of the amount collected as premiums are met by government subsidy. The
principle of subsidy is established partly in recognition of the nation's
responsibility to participate in costs incurred for benefite which accrue
to the nation, and partly to keep premiums within the farmers' capacity
to pay.
d. The Matural Resources Section assisted the Ministry of
Agriculture and Forestry officiale in preparing the present crop and
livestock insurance legielation, in developing and implementing adminie-
trative policies, and in analyzing the progress of insurance activities
under the new law.
III. FISHING INDUSTRY
1. Introduction: a. The Japanese. to a greater extent than any
other major nation. have developed the resources of the sea to supply
their neede. This development has been stimulated by the very limited
land areas suitable for cultivation, the pressure of population, and the
need of this population for food. and by the concentration of the popu-
lation on the shores of productive seas with steep mountains at their
back and the open seas before them. More than one million people are
engagod full or part time in exploiting the resources of the sea. Practi-
cally every speciee of sen animal and plant is used for human consumption
or for industrial purposes, and the Japanese fishermen before World War
II had spread out to fish a greater area of the high seas than was ever
before exploited by one nation.
b. In prewar years. the production of Japanese fishermen operating
from the home islande averaged about 9.300,000,000 pounds, This amount
10

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.99)
was more than double the production of the United states, the second most
important fish producing nation of the world. About 7,900,000,000 pemeds
of Japen's prewar production came from the areas now authorized for Jap-
anese fishing, with most of the remainder coming from northern areas
(which are now under the administration of U.S.S.R.) and smaller qumti-
ties from the Yellow Sea and high seas outside the present area. Bwem
though Japanese fishing boats ranged widely aver the high seas about 85
percent of Japen's total production came from waters lying within sight
of the coast of the four main islande.
2. Recent Productioni a. During the last part of World War II
Japanese fishery production decreased greatly as a result of shortages
of petroleum for operating fishing boats, shortages of fishing gear, less
of the larger fishing boats taken over by the Japanese navy. and disrup-
tion of the handling and distribution of fish on shore as a result of
bombing attacks. No dependable data are available for the amount of fish
produced during l945, which was the low point in fish production, but it
was far below the prewar level.
b. Following the surrender, fish production was rapidly in-
creased by the construction of new fishing boats and the distribusien ef
petroleum and of fishing gear made up from manila and cotton fibers re-
leased by the Japenese armed forces when they were demobilised. By 1947
production had been restored to a level approaching the prewar average
for the same area, except for sardines and herring. Before the war these
species supplied about 3,000,000,000 pounds to the annual cateh, but in
recent years their abundance in the sea has decreased, for reasons not
understood by fishermen or scientists. This decline in the sardime runs
has been paralleled on the coast of California where little more is known
about the cause than in the Japanese fisheries.
c. By 1948 the Japanese fishing fleet had been restored to its
prewar level, but supolies of fishing ropes, nets, and twine produced
mostly from manila and cotton left by the Japanese armed foroes had been
nearly exhausted, and current imports of these materials were far short
of requirements. As a result of this shortage of supplies and the con-
tinued scarcity of sardines, aquatic production in 1948 dropped te about
6,000,000,000 pounds, a level considerably below that of the previous
yoar.
3. Fishing Supplies: a. The growing scarcity of fishing supplies
first becmme evident during field surveys made by fishery experte of
Natural Besources Section in the spring and summer of 1947, to determine
the status of fish production. These specialists estimated that pro-
duction would drop at least 25 percent in 1948 and more in the following
years unless funds for purchese of such materials vere greatly increased.
As a result of this warning, an emergency allotment of more than
$15,000,000 was made available late in 1947, and additional funds total-
ing nearly $45,000,000 were spent for fisheries supplies during the
following year. After these funds became available, ray materials,
consisting mostly of manila and cotton fibers and petroleum producte.
were purchased and brought into Japan where thay were processed and
began reaching the fishermen in rapidly increasing quantities during
the fall of l948. As a result of thie program, sufficient quantities
of materials reached the fishermen during l949 to replace most of their
old worn-out gear. With thie restoration of gear, production in 1949
is expected to approach 7,000,000,000 pounds, the highest level since
hostilities ended and considerably more than prewer production from this
area, except for sardines and herring. The large quantities of materials
distributed in l949 have done much to restore the fisherman's equipment
to normal condition so that requirements for l950 and the eneuing years
will drop to the level required for normal replacements.
11

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.99)
b. With adequate supplies of fishing materials and further
development of resources where research shows this to be pessible, pre-
duction of aquatic products in 1950-5l is expected to increase progres-
sively. If sardines and herring return to the Japanese waters in numbers
comparable to those found before the war, production will considerably
exceed the quantities taken during prevar years from the area now opem
to fishing.
c. The current Natural Resourcee Section program in the field
of fisheries supplies requires continued checking, through field trips.
on Japanese requirements for fishing materials, expediting action to ob-
tain adequate funds and procure needed supolies, and maintaining sur-
veillance over dietribution to see that materials reach the fishermsm
and are most effectively used. Surveillance is being continued over the
Japanese fishing boat building program to insure that an adequate number
and tonnage of boats are available to meet changing needs. At present
most categories of Japanese fishing boats include somewhet larger nmbers
than were in service before the war. The entire povered fleet totals
450,000 boats of 1,100,000 gross tons. In order to avoid exhaustion of
the present fishing grounds and inefficient use of limited fishing ma-
terials, no new construction is being authorised except for replacement
purposes. If development of new fisheries or extension of the present
fishing area results in a need for more boats, suitable increases in the
fishing fleet will be authorised.
4. Fishing Areas: a. The problem of fishing areas is extremely
important to Japan, not only because the area available to Japanese
fishermen affects the quantity of fish produced but alse because the use
of high seas areas by the Japanese is of much concern to the people of
the Allied powers and other neighboring nations. The original limitation
of fishing area was placed on Japenese fishing as a security measure and
to prevent complications which might arise from the contacts of Japemese
fishermen with those of other nations. Limitation on area has been con-
tinued for these reasons and also to give the Japanese fishermen an
opportunity to demonstrate whether they had changed some of the attitudes
and activities which had made Japanese prewar fishing objectionable to
the people of many other nations. These activities included over-
exploitation of fishing grounds and failure to observe international
agreements and practices.
b. Matural Besources Section personnel are advising and en-
couraging the Japsnese Government and fishing industry in eliminating
these objectionable characteristics. Violations of the authorised
fishing area have occurred, but at present the Japanese Government is
setting up and operating a fishery inspection system to detect and re-
port boate fishing outside of the authorised areas. The research pro-
gran conducted by the government is being reorganised to increase its
efficiency and effectiveness; it is being redirected to place greatest
emphasis on determining methods for administering the fisheries in order
to provide the largest possible sustained production. This attitude
contrasts with the prewar programs which stressed exploitation and ex-
pansion of fishing grounds and processing of products, with little or
no work on sustained yield. Under the new program the Japanese Govern-
ment has begun an extensive research on the fisheries of the Tast China
Sea to determine the degree of exploitation which will result in highest
sustained yield. Results so far indicate that the present trawling
fleet is considerably larger than the area can support. A program was
inaugurated in the summer of 1949 to reduce the flest by 350 boats.
Additionel reductions will be msde later if further research indicates
that the fleet is still too large.
12

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
c. In recagnition of the progress made by the Japanese Gevern-
ment and the fishing industry in reducing the violations of the authorised
fishing area and correcting over-oxploitation where it had been shown to
exist, the Supreme Commander on 19 September 1949 authorised the Japanese
Government to extend the area of Japanese fishing operations to the east
as far as the international date line. This was the first extensiem
authorised since the susmer of 1946. It indicates that the Japanese
Government is cerrecting those abuses which lead to intensive oppesition
of other nations to the expansion of fishing areas. (mn Chart 3)
5. Processing and Sanitation: Advice and guidance are being pro-
vided the Japanese by MR specialists on methode of handling and precessing
aquatic products to improve quality and eliminate spoilage and ether
wastage. Outstanding examples of this progras in operation are the
Antarotic whaling expeditions authorised by the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers in 1946, 1947, and 1948. Japanese Antarctic whaling oper-
ations before World War II paid little attention to international oon-
ventions which protected small whales and female whales. Processing
methods used did not obtain maximm returne of food producte from the
whales. The post-surrender whaling expeditions have been under the
direct supervision of SCaP representatives who have required that maxi-
mum use be made of all parts of the whales and that all international
conventions be observed. This supervision plus the techmical advice
supplied have resulted in increesing the yield per blue whale unit (eme
blue whale or the equivalent) from the l8-ton average of the Japanese
1940-1941 expedit1on to about 48 tons for the 1948-1949 expedition.
Japanese observance of internationsl conventions regarding sise of
whales, and use of whale parts, also has been exemplary.
6. Democratisation: a. Japanese fisheries operate in a far
different manner than those of the United States, where anyone can nter
most fisheries if he hes the finances to purchase equipment. In Japan
most fisheries are operated under a system of fishing rights which
functions in a menner not greetly different from land ownership in agri-
culture. These rights have been developed over the centuries as a re-
sult of the feudalistic system and of the population pressure which has
forced the people to seek new sources for food production. Under the
conditione existent in Japan, these rights have desirable features for
they help prevent over-exploitation and exhanstion of aquatic resources.
However, these righte also have led to abuses similar to those found in
absentee ownership and sharecropping in agriculture.
b. Another development under the Japanese system was the
establishment of a series of fisheries control associations, with only
one association allowed in each community. These organisations were
dominated by the government and by local village bosses. After a long
period of study by NR experts working with t he Japenese Government.
corrective legislation was developed to eliminate the old control as-
sociations and set up procedures for the operation of cooperatives
based on the democratic procedures found to be effective in western
countries. After public hearings on the proposed legislation it was
passed by the Diet under pressure from fishermen's grouve. This legis-
lation allows fiahermen to organise one or more cooperatives in each
producing area; memberehip is restricted to active fishermen, and secret
balloting with one vote per member is required. Persons not eligible
for membership in the cooperatives may utilize their services but do
not have the right to vote. By September 1949, 89 percent of the 3,131
fishery associations were in the process of dissolution and Bll cooper-
atives had been formed, with many additional cooperatives awaiting anly
formal administrative approval of their applications. It is expected
that about 6,000 cooperatives will be formed to replace the 3,131
associations, and formation will be substantially completed by 1 July
1950.

7. Administrative Organisation: The Japanese Government and the
fishing industry have been encouraged through example and advice to uti-
lise technically trained persons in government jobs dealing with the
fishing industry. As a result of this program the Japanese Fisheries
Bureau has been reorganised as a Fisheries Agency. All of the afficials
of this Agency, except for the administrative personnel, have techmical
training in the fishing industry for government. They ere graimally re-
organising the Agency to promote more efficient operations and to werk
closely with the industry on problems of greatest impertance to develep-
ment of fishing. This change is obtaining incre-sing support frem members
of the industry but meets with opposition from some quarters for it rep-
resents a reversel of the old Japanese concept that the industry
functions for the benefit of the government.
IV. WILDLIFE
The wildlife of Japan supplies a considerable amount of animal
protein to the rural population in areas where such proteine are most
needed. Nost hunting rights are owned by hunting associations and are
operated to provide game for market rather than recreation. Fnod short-
ages during the war caused excessive killing, resulting in deereases in
wildlife so great that even song birds are rarely seen. Furthermore the
control of harmful insects by birds has been greatly reduced. The Jap-
anese have been encouraged and advised in revising their game laws to
provide protection for beneficial birds and other wildlife and to make
possible the maximum sustained yield from these resources, whether for
commercial shooting or sport. They alen have been assisted in studies
of fur seals to provide information needed in development of interna-
tional treaties bearing on the exploitation of fur seals.
V. POREST CONDITIONS
1. Introduction:' a. Although Japan has the highest ratio be-
tween forest area and cultivated land of any country outside of the
tropics and Finland, it is faced with a serious forest situation.
Stating the problem in its simplest terms, Japen's timber is being cut
much faster than it is being replaced through growth.
b. Forests occupy 68 percent of the total land aree and are
of paramount importance in the general land use pattern of the country.
The resources of these forests are vital to t he national welfare through
their contribution to a self-sustaining economy. Foreste are especially
vital to the Japenese people as a source of fuel and timber since per
capita consumption of wood in Japen is one of the highest in the world.
Becsuse of the extremely mountainous terrain which requires forest over
as watershed protection, the forests have an important influence on food
and power production; they regulate water supply, contrel floods, and
prevent erosion and silting of reservoirs and farm lands.
c. Despite their vast forest areas the Japanese do not have
timber resources to meet their needs indefinitely without rigid conser-
vation measures. Present demands are being met at the expense of feture
requirements. Japen has imported wood since World War I, when this pelicy
was adopted in order to preserve the balance between use and growth.
Importation of wond contiaued, in general, until the late 1920's when
15

=== Page 334 === (confidence 0.98)
the demande of Japan's militarists made it neceseary to expend expert .
credits on more strategic war mmterials. As a result of this change is
policy, Japan's forests were subjected to overcutting to such am extemt
that during the years of World War II the drain reached four times the
amount of growth. During the Occupation, demands have continmed at a
high level. Wond has been used primarily for essential recenstructies
and rehabilitation, but, no matter how great the need, the result is
that the coniferous timber stands are being overcut at an annual rate
more than two and one-half times the growth. If this rate of over-
cutting continues, it is estimated that these important stands of timber
will be virtually exhausted within 15 years. Nevertheless, current
production plans of the Japenese call for increased cutting in forests
during the next five yeare. This situation is made even more serious by
the fact that, in successive years of overcutting, replacement of timber
through growth becomes less and less (mm Chart 4).

d. The effects of overexploitation are evident through in-
creasing dameges to agricultural areas, hydroelectric and irrigation
developments, and lines of transportation and communications, as a
result of extensive soil erosion and increesing incidence of floods.
•. Other important contributing factors to Japanese forestry
problems have been and are insufficient reforestation, inedequate re-
search and extension, and depredation of pine bark beetles. of the
forest land in Japan, less than 25 percent supports coniferous timber
stands which supoly nearly 85 percent of all timber cut, and 20 per-
cent supports broadleaved timber stands from which 15 percent of the
16

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.99)
timber is cut. Of the total forest areas at present 1l percent needs
reforestation, another ll percent is wasteland (called "genya"), and
nearly 10 percent is inaccessible.
f. There is no easy formula for the rapid correction of the
results of poor forestry practices in the pest. Trees grow slowly, and
forest progrems require many years. It is estimated that 80 to 100 years
will be required to rehabilitate Japan's forests to approach mmximom
productivity.
6. In order to shorten and bridge the gap between the existing
timber supply and the time when new resources come into harvest, the
Matural Resources Section is furnishing guidance and advice to various
Japanese Government agencies on the following specific programs: improved
silvicultural practices, reforestation, wood preservation, opening of
inaccessible forests, use of wood substitutes, improved timber utilisa-
tion processes, reduction of timber cut by importation, and embargo of
raw timber exports.
h. For more than three years, the forest situation has been
studied and data gathered by the Watural Rescurces Section. These data
illustrate the complete reliance of the Japanese on their forests. They
show the inadequacy of present forest production and the urgent need for
remedial action. On the basis of this information the Natural Resources
Section is now taking steps to formulate an over-all forest conservation
policy, and through guidance and assistance to the Japanese to establish
adequate management plans as a partial solution to the forest and timber
supply problems. Progress has been made during the past yeer in the
development of programs in forestry education, research, and extension.
2. Puelwond Forests: a. Fuelwond forests present an important
problem which is not generally recognised. These forests are, in the
main, the result of clearcutting broadleaved forests. Regrowth has been
mostly by stump sprouting and the resulting stands are known as coppice
or brush forests. The creation of coppice forests and their contimued
cutting without regard to potential productivity have resulted in serious
forest and soil deterioration. Without special silvicultural treatment,
coppice forests produce only low grade timber and that at a reduced rate.
The important problem of the fuelwood forest is to increase productivity
on a portion of the area to the demand level, by special cultural treat-
ment. The remainder of the area can be converted to coniferous stands,
which will result in greatly increased timber production. The pro-
ductivity of the fuelwood forests of Japan is a minor fraction of the
maximam possible; it can be increased to three to five times the present
yield when recommended prectices have been implemented fully.
b. Efforts are being made to incresse yields of these fuel-
wood forests. Forest legislation providing for improved forest manage-
ment practices on private forest lands is being formulated. Under the
Visiting Expert Program, a forest management specialist is being obtained
to analyse the hardwood forest situation and make specific recommen-
dations to this end.
3. Managepent Practices: Better silvicultural practices can
improve the timber supply situation immediately. Hajor advances can be
made by the implementation of silvicultural practices which have beem
recommended by Matural Resources Section foresters. The institution
of selective cutting practices on private forest lands is particularly
important in Japen, where the existing method of forest management is to
clearout the timber stands. A serious flond and erosion problem has
resulted from this practice. Records indicate that enormous losses have
17

=== Page 336 === (confidence 0.97)
occurred through dammge to food cropè and soil. kresiem ef ferest sails
is resulting in deterioration of site quality. silting in ef reserveirs
is causing dasage to hydroelectric installations and redacing sterage
capacity of water for irrigation and demestic use. The applicatiem of
selective cutting practices to the timber stands will immediately prevems
an increase in the serionsmess of this situation. Adeptien ef this cat-
ting system would immediately arrest the enlargement of she area is meed
of reforestation, increase the growth rate of existing stands to many
times their present production, materially reduce loss throagh seil
erosion and floods, and conserve forest soil resources. Legislatiem is
being formulated by the Japanese to provide for these impreved ferest
management practices on all forest lands.
4. Reforestations The need for reforestation is recogaised by
foreaters to be one of Japan's most important national preblems. At
present, nearly 8,000,000 acres need reforesting. With  gaidamee.
plans have been prepared by the Forestry Agency, in ceoperatiem with
prefectural officials and private owners, to reforest these 8,000,000
acres. Reforestation of these lands will contribute substamtially te-
ward solving the national timber supply problem. In additiem to the
above program, a five-year school reforestation pregram emvisagins the
planting of 250,000 acres of trees by school childrem was laamohed in
1949.
5. Insect Control: Another serions problem in Japam is the
elimination of ertensive timber losses resulting frem iasect infestatiems.
Several species of pine bark beetles in Japan are taking a heavy tell ef
the pine timber. Losses from insect damage more then offeet the total

7. Utilizetion of Materiala: Relatively complete utilisation of
raw forest products is practiced in Japan. Slashings from logging opera-
tions and materials which cannot be used for the production of merohant-
able timber are used for firewood or converted into charcoal. Hovwever,
improvement of millinz processes is definitely needed in Japan. Lack of
proper practices frequently results in logs being used for purposes which
do not utilize their maximum worth. With NR guidance and encouragement,
the Japanese are attempting to use sawmill waste for the production of
alcohol and yeast by the process of wood saccharification. Research on
wood saccharification to investigate the lstest technologies for the
production of alcohol and yeast is being conducted at several universi-
ties. Three industrial concerns have installed pilot plant equipment
for wood saccharification and have indicated their intention to install
large-scale operations, providing experimental operations prove succese-
ful. Two of the largest pulp and paper mills located in Hokkaido have
installed equipment for the production of alcohol from sulfite weste
liquors. These units were put into operstion early in l949. Ome mill
has a capacity of 360 kiloliters of 95 percent ethyl alcohol per year
and the other a capacity of 600 kiloliters per yeer. These developments
represent distinct progress in improving the utilization of timber
resourcss.
8. Wood pulps The wood chemical industry is of major importance
to the economy of Japan. This industry not only provides all of the
paper and paper products so important to the welfare of the people, but
has the potential ability to supply et least 50 percent of their textile
requirements. The principal raw material for this industry is timber.
which is being consumed at a rate far exceeding its replacement throngh
growth. Teohmologists for the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
have been instrumental in improving the efficiency of the use of woed
for the manufacture of wood pulp. Improved technology has resulted is
increasing wood pulp yields about 30 percent using the same amount of
raw material with no changes in processing plants. Further advice and
guidance in the utilization of rew materials, by improving techmology
for the use of broadleaved species and the recovery of by-products, is
necessary to increase utilization of timber resources for wood pulp.

VI. MINING AND GBOLOGY
1. Introduction: a. Two major requirements in an indastrial
econony are a supply of minerals and metals and a source of emergy which
can provide adequate power for industrial operation. On the basis of
estimated l950 requirements, the mines of Japan are providing at current
rates of production one-sixth of its copper; ene-seventh of its lead;
about seven-eighths of its sinc; less than one-fifth of its iron ore; ene-
half of its sulfur; five-sixths of its coal; and about one-seventh of its
petroleum. It is estimated that production of coal, pyrite, sulfur, and
possibly sinc can be increased to meet full requirements, with some sur-
plus for export, and that Japan can preduce three-fourths of the cepper
it needs, and lesser amounts of other minerals. The greatest possible
utilization of indigenous minerals is extremely isportant, mot only be-
cause foreign exchange will be limited and the United States will have
to finance imports, but because for some years Japan probably will be
foroed to buy on a seller's market. Available foreign supplies will mot
be plentiful.
b. Although Japan's requirements' are far in excess of its pro-
duction of minerals and metals, because of the fuel and power shortage
the actual demand for mine products has been, in some instances, less
than production. As industrial activity continues to increase, the de-
mand for mine products will grow. The Natural Resources Section has
attempted to place the mining industry in a position to meet this dmand
when it does come. Improved drilling and blasting methods have been
introduced; changes have been suggested in stoping methods to cut dewn
the amount of waste rock being mined in narrow veins in Japan's ore de-
posits; developpent work, which was seriously neglected during the war,
has been encouraged; new mining techniques have been advocated to raise
efficiency in Japanese mines.
2. Metallurgy: In the metallurgical industry, operating as it is
at but a fraction of capacity because of power, fuel, and raw material
shortages, the principal step which can be taken is to prepare to meet a
rising demand for the products of the industry by improving techmical
nethods. Possibilities for improvement are exemplified by a copper re-
finery in Tochigi, where introduction of an improved method in the final
step of copper refining reduced fuel consumption 27 percent, while the
purity of copper produced was increased. Improved techniques such as
this can do much to reduce the fuel shortage.
3. Petroleam: a. Japan's production of crude oil from its own
wells has never been much over 10 percent of its total crude oil require-
ments. However, any petroleum produced in Japan at least reduces the

quaptity which mast be imported. Furthermore, indigemous oil is important
because several types of special purpose oils are derived almost entirely
from Japanese sources. At the beginning of the Occupatiom almost all of
the kmown potential oil producing areas had been drilled, and the explo-
ratien program was falling far short of meeting the need for new reserves
of petrolenm. As an example of the situatien that existed, the 1946-47
exploration and exploitation drilling program was bringing only one suo-
cessful producing well out of every twelve which were drilled. This
condition resulted from the lack of acceptable basic information. This
is particularly true of the exploitation drilling program which merely
involves drilling wells in fields which suppesedly are thoroughly mapped.
As a result of the entire 1946-47 program, only 20,000 kileliters of now
reserves vere discovered, slightly more than one month'e production at
current rates.
b. Under guidance of scap, a long-range petroleum exploration
program was set up by the Petroleum Resources Development Premotion
Council, formerly the Petroleum Erploration Advancement Committee. The
Council, which wes organised in January l947, is composed of leading
petroleum geologists from the Imperial Oil Company, Japanese Geelogical
Sarvey, Mining Bureau, and Japanese universities. As a direct result of
the work of the committee in guiding the petrolens program, is 1947-48
one out of three exploitation wells was successful, and one out of six
exploratien wells was a producer. Two small new fields with receverable
reserves of some 200,000 kiloliters of petrolem were discovered. A more
significant development in the petroleum picture is the discovery that
petrolenm production may possibly be ebtained in Hokkaido frem large
areas previously overlooked by the Japanese. Nuch of the 1948-49 progrem

4. Pyrites: a. Pyrites are used to produce sulfuric acid which
in turn is used ia Japan to manufacture two principal fertilisers, super-
phosphate and ammonilm sulfate (nitrogenous fertiliser), as well as sther
important products. During World War II, pprite mines were exploited by
overproduction and neglect of development and maintenance. The most
accessible ore was mined, and back filling was neglected, leaving large
ore reserves unavailable for immediate extraction. Nachinery and equip-
ment deteriorated from lack of necessary repairs and replacements.
b. In 1946, domestic pyrite production was 630,000 metric tons.
With guidance from the Matural Resources Section, production was raised
to 1,200,000 metric tons in 1948 and is expected to rise to approximately
1,540,000 metric tons in the 1951 fiscal year. The mmnual needs for
pyrite are about 1,700,000 metric tons, so imports have been necessary
to supplement indigenous preduction. The increased efficiency obtained
through improved mining methods introduced by American experts thms has
reduced the imports needed to sustain the Japanese economy. Eventually
Japan is expected to be able to provide enough pyrite from domestic
sources to eliminate the need for further imports.
5. Phosphate Rock: a. Japan has negligible supplies of the raw
material (phosphate rock) which, in addition to pyrites, is needed to
preduce superphosphate fertiliser for agricultural uses. A survey of
the nearest and most dependable sources of phosphate rock indicated that
Angaur Island in the Palau Group has the most available deposits.
b. The Angaur Project, which includes the mining, drying, and
shipping to Japan of phosphate reck from the island, therefore was organ-
ised under the supervision of the Eatural Resources Section and has sup-
plied a substantial proportion of Japaa's requirements since 1947.

c. The only other source of phosphate rock fer Japam weale.
require the expenditure of United States funds for the outrighe purehase

6. Coking Coal: Another important program bas as ite primoipal
objective making Japan self-sufficient in coking coal for productien of
metallurgical coke for use in manufacturing iron and steel. During the
past year, coking coal valued at $50,000,000 has been imported from the
United States, because Japanese coal is of such a high volatile nature
that it must be blended with other low volatile coals in order to pre-
duce a usable grade of metallurgical coke. Visiting specialists frem
the United States are assisting in supervision of coke-making techmiques
which will permit use of a much smaller percentage of imported ceal with
the indigenous product.
7. Mine Safety: a. Before and during the war, the mines of Japam
were owned by individuals or corporations and operated with beavy gevera-
ment subsidies. The emphasis was on production with little attentiem
paid to safety practices. Injury and damage were considered an expemse
necessary to production, and such costs were included in the price of
material produced, but the problem was otherwise neglected. Japaaese
miners had little idea of safety, and mine operators neglected what we
consider to be their responsibility for the safety of workers. Conse-
quently, accident rates vere appal'ing, many miners forsook their jebs
to take up less hasardeus pursuits, and the skille gained by experience
were lost through a large turnover of employees.
b. After a study of these conditions, Matural Resourees
Section mining experts recommended mine safety codes, and the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers placed responsibility on the Japanese
Government for implementing these codes. Guided by American experts.
the Japanese established a well-staffed, competent agency to handle the
functioning of the mine safety codes. À groving safety conscieusmess
on the part of both management and labor is already evident. Accidemt
rates which in 1946 were 50 deaths and 2,934 injuries per million metrie
tons of coal produced already have dropped to about 20 deaths and 1,119
injuries per million metric tons. In the United States in 1945 these
rates were 1.72 deaths and 82.9 injuries per million tons of coal pre-
duced. The mine safety codes have not been in operation long emeugh te
approach maximum effectiveness. Hovever, the results to date indicate
that conditions already are so wuch better that much greater imprevememt
can be anticipated. Eventually safety records comparable to those of
the United States may be achieved.
8. Mining Laws: Matural Resources Section technicians and experts
also have advised the Japanese Government regarding the objectives ef
affording greater opportunities for individuals to engage in miming
operations and thereby extend the development of mineral resources:
ending monopolistic practices by shortening the period of prespecting
rights and requiring actual development work by those desiring to retais
mining claims; and establishing a more effective means of settling die-
putes between farmers and mine operators for damage to cultivated land by

mendations is not complete, work is well under way and excellent results
are anticipated.
9. Summery: Through assistance and advice to the Japanese, the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers is attempting to maximise the
production of needed minerals, metals, and petroleum and to introduce
more efficient methods which will promote more effective use of Japen's
scarce supplies. Some of the programs are desigmed to insure maximum
long-term production in addition to increasing the immediate supply.
Results to date have shown that this progran is a sound one.
